Flow equation approach to singular stochastic PDEs
Paweł Duch, 04103 Leipzig, Germany, Universität Leipzig, 04103 Leipzig, Germany

Abstract
We prove universality of a macroscopic behavior of solutions of a large class of semi-linear parabolic SPDEs on ℝ + × 𝕋 \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T} with fractional Laplacian ( − Δ ) σ / 2 (-\Delta)^{\sigma/2} , additive noise and polynomial non-linearity, where 𝕋 \mathbb{T} is the 𝖽 \mathsf{d} -dimensional torus. We consider the weakly non-linear regime and not necessarily Gaussian noises which are stationary, centered, sufficiently regular and satisfy some integrability and mixing conditions. We prove that the macroscopic scaling limit exists and has a universal law characterized by parameters of the relevant perturbations of the linear equation. We develop a new solution theory for singular SPDEs of the above-mentioned form using the Wilsonian renormalization group theory and the Polchinski flow equation. In particular, in the case of 𝖽 = 4 \mathsf{d}=4 and the cubic non-linearity our analysis covers the whole sub-critical regime σ > 2 \sigma>2 . Our technique avoids completely all the algebraic and combinatorial problems arising in different approaches. MSC classification: 60H17, 81T17



Contents

1
Introduction

The aim of this paper is to investigate the large-scale behavior of solutions of semi-linear parabolic stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) of the form

\mathbf{Q}\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Phi}}_{\nu}(x)=\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{F}}}_{\nu}[\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Phi}}_{\nu}](x),\qquad x=(\mathring{x},\bar{x})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}},

(1.1)

with an appropriate initial condition at time
\mathring{x}=0
, where

\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{F}}}_{\nu}[\varphi](x):=\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathsf{d}m}}\lambda^{i}\,\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,m,a}_{\nu}~\partial^{a_{1}}\varphi(x)\ldots\partial^{a_{m}}\varphi(x).

(1.2)

We make the following assumptions:

•

\mathsf{d}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
is the spatial dimension of the spacetime
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
.

•

\mathbf{Q}
is a parabolic (pseudo-)differential operator of the form
\partial_{\mathring{x}}+(-\Delta_{\bar{x}})^{\sigma/2}
, where
(-\Delta_{\bar{x}})^{\sigma/2}
is the fractional Laplacian of order
\sigma\in(0,\mathsf{d})
.

•

\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
is some constant and
\nu\in(0,1]
is the scaling parameter.

•

\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
is some centered and stationary random field on
\mathbb{M}
that is sufficiently regular and satisfies some mixing and integrability conditions.

•

The parameters
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
are non-zero only for finitely many
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})
such that the spatial multi-indices
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}
satisfy the condition
|a_{1}|,\ldots,|a_{m}|<\sigma
(the last condition says that the SPDE (
1.1
) is semi-linear).

The SPDE (
1.1
) is called the microscopic equation, its RHS denoted by
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{F}}}_{\nu}[\varphi]
is called the microscopic force, the random field
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
is called the microscopic noise and the parameters
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,m,a}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}
are called microscopic force coefficients. The microscopic equation is supposed to describe some self-interacting system influenced by a random environment modeled by the noise
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
.

Definition 1.1
.

For
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
we define
[\nu]:=|\nu|^{1/\sigma}
.

We consider the so-called weakly non-linear regime. We allow the non-linear terms in the microscopic equation to depend explicitly on the scaling parameter
\nu\in(0,1]
and demand that they vanish in the limit
\nu\searrow 0
. More specifically, we suppose that
|\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,m,a}_{\nu}|\lesssim[\nu]^{i\dim(\lambda)-\varepsilon}
uniformly in
\nu\in(0,1]
for some
\dim(\lambda)>0
and all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.

In order to avoid technical problems we investigate the microscopic equation on a finite spatial domain. More specifically, we assume that the microscopic noise
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
is
2\pi/[\nu]
periodic in space. In particular, it depends on the scaling parameter
\nu
. However, the dependence of
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
on
\nu
is assumed to be very weak.

Definition 1.2
.

To every quantity
X
we assign a certain parabolic dimension
\dim(X)\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular, for
x=(\mathring{x},\bar{x})=(\mathring{x},\bar{x}^{1},\ldots,\bar{x}^{\mathsf{d}})\in\mathbb{M}
we define
\dim(\mathring{x})=-\sigma
,
\dim(\bar{x}^{k})=-1
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{d}\}
. The parabolic dimension of the spacetime
\mathbb{M}
equals
-\mathsf{D}:=-\sigma-\mathsf{d}
. We assume that

\dim(\lambda)>0,\quad\dim(\varPhi):=(\mathsf{d}-\sigma)/2>0,\quad\dim(\varXi):=(\mathsf{d}+\sigma)/2>0.

(1.3)

Remark 1.3
.

The constant
\lambda
plays a role of a bookkeeping parameter that can be set equal to
1
at the end of the construction. In particular, the dimension of
\lambda
need not coincide with the physical dimension.

Definition 1.4
.

The set
\bar{\mathfrak{I}}
consists of all triples
(i,m,a)
such that
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})
, where
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}
are spatial multi-indices such that
|a_{1}|,\ldots,|a_{m}|<\sigma
. For
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}
we define

\varrho(i,m,a):=-\dim(\varXi)+i\dim(\lambda)+m\dim(\varPhi)+|a|,\qquad|a|=|a_{1}|+\ldots+|a_{m}|.

(1.4)

The sets
\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
consist of triples
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}
such that
\varrho(i,m,a)\leq 0
and
\varrho(i,m,a)>0
, respectively. The force coefficients
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
with
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{\mp}
are called relevant/irrelevant. Because by assumption
\dim(\lambda)>0
and
\dim(\varPhi)>0
there are only finitely many relevant coefficients.

Since the noise
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
is assumed to be regular the initial value problem for the microscopic equation is classically well-posed (in some open possibly random time interval). Let
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Phi}}_{\nu}
be the solution of the microscopic equation (
1.1
). In the paper we investigate the limit
\nu\searrow 0
of the stochastic process
\varPhi_{\nu}
related to the process
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Phi}}_{\nu}
by the equation

\varPhi_{\nu}(\mathring{x},\bar{x})=[\nu]^{-\dim(\varPhi)}\,\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Phi}}_{\nu}(\mathring{x}/\nu,\bar{x}/[\nu]).

(1.5)

Note that the rescaled process
\varPhi_{\nu}
satisfies the following equation

\mathbf{Q}\varPhi_{\nu}(x)=F_{\nu}[\varPhi_{\nu}](x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}},

(1.6)

with the initial condition
\varPhi_{\nu}(0,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})=\phi_{\nu}
, where

F_{\nu}[\varphi](x):=\varXi_{\nu}(x)+\sum_{(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}}\,\lambda^{i}\,f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}~\partial^{a_{1}}\varphi(x)\ldots\partial^{a_{m}}\varphi(x)

(1.7)

and

\displaystyle\varXi_{\nu}(\mathring{x},\bar{x}):=[\nu]^{-\dim(\varXi)}\,\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}(\mathring{x}/\nu,\bar{x}/[\nu]),

(1.8)

\displaystyle f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}:=[\nu]^{-\dim(\varXi)+m\dim(\varPhi)+|a|}\,\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,m,a}_{\nu}.

(1.9)

The SPDE (
1.6
) is called the macroscopic equation, its RHS denoted by
F_{\nu}[\varphi]
is called the (macroscopic) force, the random field
\varXi_{\nu}
is called the (macroscopic) noise and the parameters
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}
are called the (macroscopic) force coefficients.

Note that the rescaled noise
\varXi_{\nu}
is
2\pi
periodic in space. We assume that the initial data
\phi_{\nu}
is also periodic and look for periodic solutions of the macroscopic equation. Consequently, the macroscopic equation can be equivalently viewed as an equation on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}
, where
\mathbb{T}
is the
\mathsf{d}
-dimensional torus of size
2\pi
.

The microscopic force coefficients vanish in the limit
\nu\searrow 0
. The same is true for the irrelevant macroscopic force coefficients. On the other hand, the relevant macroscopic force coefficients often do not have a limit as
\nu\searrow 0
.

Under our assumptions about the noise
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
the law of
\varXi_{\nu}
converges to the law of the white noise
\varXi_{0}
on
\mathbb{H}:=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
as
\nu\searrow 0
. Moreover,
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}=0
for all irrelevant force coefficients. This suggests that formally the macroscopic process defined by the scaling limit
\varPhi_{0}:=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\nu}
should satisfy the following SPDE driven by the white noise

\mathbf{Q}\varPhi_{0}(x)=\varXi_{0}(x)+\sum_{(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}}\lambda^{i}\,f^{i,m,a}_{0}~\partial^{a_{1}}\varPhi_{0}(x)\ldots\partial^{a_{m}}\varPhi_{0}(x).

(1.10)

Observe that the sum on the RHS of the above equation involves only the relevant force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{0}=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
. However, the coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{0}
need not be well-defined or finite. The regularity of the solution of the above equation is expected to be slightly worse than
-\dim(\varPhi)=(\sigma-\mathsf{d})/2
, which is negative by assumption. As a result, the products that appear in the above SPDE are typically ill-defined and the equation is generically singular (apart from the spacial cases when it happens to be linear).

Remark 1.5
.

In order to make sense of a singular SPDE of the form (
1.10
) one considers its approximations. Any macroscopic SPDE of the form (
1.6
) is a valid approximation. The standard approximation of a singular SPDE of the form (
1.10
) is obtained by replacing the white noise
\varXi_{0}
with a mollified noise
\varXi_{\nu;0}
and allowing the force coefficients
f_{\nu;0}^{i,m,a}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
, to depend on the UV cutoff
\nu\in(0,1]
. It turns out the standard approximation is of the form (
1.6
).

Remark 1.6
.

Because of the assumption
\dim(\lambda)>0
the singular SPDEs obtained from the macroscopic equations (
1.6
) in the limit
\nu\searrow 0
are always sub-critical in the sense of
[
26
,
4
]
.

The main result of the paper is the proof of the existence and universality of the macroscopic scaling limit
\varPhi_{0}=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\nu}
, where the process
\varPhi_{\nu}
is obtained by the rescaling (
1.5
) of the solution
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Phi}}_{\nu}
of the microscopic equation (
1.1
). We assume the following:

(1)

The noise is stationary, centered and satisfies certain regularity, integrability and mixing conditions.

(2)

The initial data is sufficiently close to equilibrium (in the sense explained below).

(3)

The relevant microscopic force coefficients have a specific asymptotic behavior for small values of the scaling parameter
\nu
.

Our assumptions about the noise are quite typical and essentially coincide with those introduced in
[
25
]
. Recall that in the case of singular SPDEs close to criticality in general it is not possible to start the equation from initial data of the same regularity as the solution
[
4
]
. The need for the above-mentioned fine-tuning of the relevant force coefficients is called the renormalization problem.

The solution manifold of the macroscopic SPDE (
1.6
) can be parameterized in terms of the initial data
\phi_{\nu}
and the force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
. In order to solve the renormalization problem we use a different parametrization that is well defined in the limit
\nu\searrow 0
and involves certain macroscopically measurable quantities like, for example, the effective mass at a spatial scale of order one.

Let
G\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
be the fundamental solution for the pseudo-differential operator
\mathbf{Q}
. We call
G
the fractional heat kernel. Note that
G
is supported in
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
and is smooth outside the hyperplane
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
(if
\sigma\in 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
, then
G
is smooth outside the origin). We define a differentiable family of smooth kernels
G_{\mu}
,
\mu\in[0,\infty)
in such a way that
G_{\mu}
coincides with
G
in the time interval
[2\mu,\infty)
and its support is contained in the time interval
[\mu,\infty)
. Note that the characteristic spatial length scale of the kernel
G_{\mu}
is of order
[\mu]=\mu^{1/\sigma}
and
G_{0}=G
.

Remark 1.7
.

The fact that for
\sigma\notin 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
the fractional heat kernel is not smooth on the hyperplane
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
, which is not a compact set, poses a major difficulty. Our analysis can be significantly simplified in the case
\sigma\in 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
, that is, when the SPDE under investigation is expressed in terms of local differentiable operators.

We claim that under the assumption about the initial data stated below the renormalization problem for the initial value formulation of the macroscopic SPDE (
1.6
) is equivalent to the renormalization problem for the following equation

\varPsi_{\nu}=(G-G_{1})\ast F_{\nu}[\varPsi_{\nu}]

(1.11)

posed in the whole space
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
. The fundamental object of the flow equation approach is the effective force functional

C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})\ni\varphi\mapsto F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}),\qquad\mu\in[0,1],

(1.12)

which is a formal power series in
\lambda
of the form

\langle F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi],\psi\rangle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\lambda^{i}\,\langle F_{\nu,\mu}^{i,m},\psi\otimes\varphi^{\otimes m}\rangle,

(1.13)

where
\psi,\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
and given
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the coefficients
F_{\nu,\mu}^{i,m}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
vanish for all but finitely many
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. The effective force coefficients
F_{\nu,\mu}^{i,m}
are constructed recursively with the use of a certain flow equation of the form

\partial_{\mu}F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}=\sum_{j=0}^{i}\sum_{k=0}^{m}(k+1)\,\mathbf{B}_{\mu}(F^{j,k+1}_{\nu,\mu},F^{i-j,m-k}_{\nu,\mu}),

(1.14)

where
\mathbf{B}_{\mu}
is some bilinear operator. By definition,
F_{\nu,0}[\varphi]
coincides with the force
F_{\nu}[\varphi]
. For
\mu,\eta\in[0,1]
such that
\eta\leq\mu
the effective force satisfies the equation

F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]=F_{\nu,\eta}[\varphi+(G_{\eta}-G_{\mu})\ast F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]].

(1.15)

This equation with
\eta=0
and
\mu=1
implies that the formal power series

\varPsi_{\nu}=(G-G_{1})\ast F_{\nu,1}[0]=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\lambda^{i}\,(G-G_{1})\ast F^{i,0}_{\nu,1}

(1.16)

is a stationary solution of Eq. (
1.11
). For a list
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})
of spatial multi-indices we define a polynomial

\mathcal{X}^{m,a}(x;x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}):=(x-x_{1})^{a_{1}}\ldots(x-x_{m})^{a_{m}}.

(1.17)

Let
1_{\mathbb{M}}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{M})
be the constant function equal to
1
. We shall prove that the formula

\langle f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu},\psi\rangle:=\langle F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu},\psi\otimes 1_{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes m}\rangle,\qquad F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}:=\mathcal{X}^{m,a}F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu},

(1.18)

defines the coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
. Note that
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,0}
coincides with the force coefficient
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}
. For
\mu>0
the coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
are measurable functions of the noise.

Definition 1.8
.

The expectation of a random variable
X
is denoted by
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}X\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
.

We are ready to state our assumptions about the force coefficients. We suppose that the irrelevant force coefficients are arbitrary such that the following bounds

|f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}|\lesssim[\nu]^{\varrho(i,m,a)-\varepsilon},\qquad(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},

(1.19)

hold uniformly in
\nu\in(0,1]
and we fix the relevant force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
, by the following renormalization conditions

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{0},\quad(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-},

(1.20)

where
\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
are arbitrarily chosen renormalization parameters. By stationarity
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
is constant over spacetime.

Remark 1.9
.

Eq. (
1.9
) implies that the bound (
1.19
) is equivalent to the bound
|\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,m,a}_{\nu}|\lesssim[\nu]^{i\dim(\lambda)-\varepsilon}
expressed in terms of microscopic force coefficients.

Remark 1.10
.

In our approach all the relevant force coefficients (including the counterterms) are fixed by the renormalization conditions and for any
\nu\in(0,1]
their values can be computed with the use of the flow equation.

Remark 1.11
.

It turns out that the bound (
1.19
) holds automatically also for the relevant force coefficients.

Remark 1.12
.

It is important that the kernel
G-G_{1}
that appears in Eq. (
1.11
) belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{M})
. Note that this is not the case for the kernel
G
.

As we mentioned above, we need to assume the initial data
\phi_{\nu}
is a small perturbation of the initial data leading to a stationary solution. More precisely, we suppose that
\varPhi_{\nu}(0,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})=\phi_{\nu}
is of the following form

\phi_{\nu}=\phi^{\triangleright}_{\nu}+\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}},\quad\phi^{\triangleright}_{\nu}:=\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}(0,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}),\quad\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}:=(G-G_{1})\ast f^{\triangleright}_{\nu},\quad f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}:=\sum_{i=0}^{i_{\triangleright}}\lambda^{i}F^{i,0}_{\nu,1},

(1.21)

where
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
are the effective force coefficients defined above. We choose
i_{\triangleright}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
in such a way that for any
i>i_{\triangleright}
the coefficient of order
\lambda^{i}
in the series (
1.16
) can be uniformly bounded in some space with positive regularity. Note that
\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}
defined above is obtained by truncating the stationary solution (
1.16
) of Eq. (
1.11
) at order
\lambda^{i_{\triangleright}}
. We assume that the random functions
\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}
are such that
\phi_{0}^{\mathrm{reg}}=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}
converges in a Besov space
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T})
with the regularity
\beta
depending on
\dim(\lambda)
and
\dim(\varPhi)
. If
\dim(\lambda)>\dim(\varPhi)
, then
\beta
coincides with the expected regularity of the macroscopic scaling limit
\varPhi_{0}=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\nu}
. If
\dim(\lambda)
is small, then
\beta
can be only slightly negative.

Now we are ready to give an informal statement of the main result of the paper. For the precise formulation we refer the reader to Theorem
3.17
.

Theorem 1.13
.

Let
\varPhi_{\nu}
be the solution of the macroscopic equation (
1.6
) with the initial data
\phi_{\nu}
. Under the assumptions described above the macroscopic scaling limit
\varPhi_{0}=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\nu}
exists and its law depends only on the renormalization parameters
\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
, and the law of
\phi_{0}^{\mathrm{reg}}=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

Remark 1.14
.

For every
\nu\in(0,1]
the law of the solution
\varPhi_{\nu}
of the macroscopic equation depends on the law of the noise
\varXi_{\nu}
, the law of the initial data
\phi_{\nu}
and the values of the relevant and irrelevant force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
.

Remark 1.15
.

The fact that the law of the scaling limit
\varPhi_{0}
depends only on the renormalization parameters
\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
, and the law of
\phi_{0}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a universality-type result. For a pedagogical introduction of the notion of the weak universality in the context of SPDEs see
[
28
]
.

Remark 1.16
.

Let us recall that by assumption
\sigma\in(0,\mathsf{d})
. Hence
\dim(\varPhi)>0
. This together with
\dim(\lambda)>0
guarantees that the number of the relevant force coefficients as well as the number of renormalization conditions is finite. Consequently, given the initial data
\phi_{0}^{\mathrm{reg}}
the law of the scaling limit
\varPhi_{0}
depends only on finitely many renormalization parameters
\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
.

Remark 1.17
.

In the case
\dim(\varPhi)\leq 0
there are infinitely many possible relevant interaction terms including terms which are not polynomials. Consequently, generically no universality result is expected to hold. The exception are physical systems with symmetries (such as the system in Example
3.37
below).

Remark 1.18
.

The technique developed in the paper allows to renormalize singular SPDEs with polynomial non-linearities also in the regime
\dim(\varPhi)=0
. Note that in this case we fix the renormalization parameters in such a way that only finitely many of them are non-zero. The generalization to non-polynomial non-linearities and the regime
\dim(\varPhi)<0
is an open problem.

Remark 1.19
.

The existence of the limit
\varPhi_{0}=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\nu}
is established only on bounded time intervals up to some possibly random finite explosion time. The global in time existence is not expected for some of the equations studied in the paper.

Remark 1.20
.

Our result provides a very general approximation theory for singular sub-critical SPDEs of the form (
1.10
) with a polynomial non-linearity and driven by the additive space-time white noise. In particular, our method covers the case of non-Gaussian approximations of the white noise.

Let us briefly describe the technique we use to establish the above result. As usual, the proof can be divided into the probabilistic and deterministic part. In the probabilistic part we show that the noise
\varXi_{\nu}\equiv f^{0,0,0}_{\nu,\mu}\in C(\mathbb{M})
as well as the relevant effective force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}\in C(\mathbb{M})
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
, introduced above, converge in law as
\nu\searrow 0
to distributional random fields

f^{i,m,a}_{0,\mu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}),\qquad(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}_{0}:=\{(0,0,0)\}\cup\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-},

(1.22)

for every
\mu\in(0,1]
and the law of the random fields
f^{i,m,a}_{0,\mu}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}_{0}
depends only on the choice of the renormalization parameters
\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{0}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
. In this step we solve the renormalization problem, that is we fix appropriately the relevant force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
. Let us remark that the collection of the relevant effective force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}_{0}
plays an analogous role to the model and the enhanced noise in the approaches to singular SPDEs based on the theory of regularity structures
[
26
]
and the paracontrolled calculus
[
22
]
, respectively. Note also that the number of the relevant effective force coefficients diverges as the SPDE approaches the critical one. Proving the convergence of the relevant effective force coefficients as
\nu\searrow 0
is the main challenge in constructing solutions of singular SPDEs close to criticality. To this end, we study cumulants of the effective force coefficients and observe that they satisfy a certain flow equation that follows from the flow equation (
1.14
). Using the flow equation we prove by induction certain uniform bounds for cumulants, which imply convergence of the relevant effective force coefficients as
\nu\searrow 0
. The renormalization problem is solved by imposing appropriate boundary conditions for the flow equation for cumulants. The correct values of the counterterms, that is the relevant force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
, are determined automatically by the flow equation and the boundary conditions. We stress that in contrast to other approaches to singular SPDEs our approach does not rely on any diagrammatical representations and, in particular, avoids the algebraic and combinatorial problems related to the so-called divergent sub-diagrams.

In the deterministic part of the proof for every
\nu\in[0,1]
we construct
\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
as a function of (generic realizations of):

•

relevant effective force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}_{0}
,
\mu\in(0,1]
,

•

irrelevant force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
and

•

the initial condition
\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
.

We prove that the above-mentioned function satisfies a certain continuity property which together with the result of the probabilistic analysis implies the existence of the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}=\breve{\varPhi}_{0}
. For
\nu\in(0,1]
the distribution
\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}
is actually a function and is related to the classical mild solution
\varPhi_{\nu}
of the macroscopic equation (
1.6
) by the formula

\varPhi_{\nu}=\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}+\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu},

(1.23)

where
\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}
was introduced in Eq. (
1.21
). The function
\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}
coincides with the mild solution of the equation

\mathbf{Q}\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}=F_{\nu}[\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}+\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}]-\mathbf{Q}\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}

(1.24)

with the initial condition
\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}(0,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})=\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. The solution of the above equation in a time interval
[t,t+T]
is written in the form

\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}=\varPhi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}+\tilde{\varPhi}_{\nu},\qquad\tilde{\varPhi}_{\nu}=(G-G_{T})\ast\tilde{F}_{\nu,T}[0]=(G-G_{T})\ast\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\lambda^{i}\,\tilde{F}^{i,0}_{\nu,T},

(1.25)

where
\varPhi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}
solves the equation
\mathbf{Q}\varPhi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}=0
with an appropriately chosen initial condition at
\mathring{x}=t
and
\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
,
i,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, are coefficients of some effective force functional
\tilde{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
. We construct the coefficients
\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
recursively using a flow equation of the form (
1.14
) and show that they can be expressed in terms of the initial data at time
\mathring{x}=t
, the relevant effective force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}_{0}
as well as the irrelevant force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
. We prove bounds for the coefficients
\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
that imply the absolute convergence of the series defining
\tilde{\varPhi}_{\nu}
for sufficiently small
T\in(0,1)
. The maximal solution
\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}
of Eq. (
1.24
) is constructed by patching together solutions in small time intervals.

Remark 1.21
.

The effective force
\tilde{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
depends on
t\in[0,\infty)
and the choice of the initial data at
\mathring{x}=t
. We stress that
\tilde{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
does not coincide with the stationary effective force
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
introduced earlier to solve the renormalization problem. However, as we shall see, in a certain region the coefficients of
\tilde{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
can be expressed in terms of the coefficients of
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
. The above-mentioned relation between the two effective force functionals plays a crucial role in the proof.

The rest of this article is organized as follows. In Sec.
2
we give an informal overview of the proof. The precise statement of the main result of the paper is given in Sec.
3
. In Sec.
4
we introduce kernels that are used to control the regularity of various distributions. We also define the scale decomposition of the fractional heat kernel. Sec.
5
contains definitions of function spaces for the effective force coefficients and their cumulants. In Sec
6
we use the Taylor theorem to decompose a distribution into a local part and a certain better behaved remainder. The material presented in Sec.
4
-
6
has a preliminary character. When first reading these sections the reader should concentrate on familiarizing with the definitions of new objects. The lemmas and theorems stated in these sections can be read later when they are referred to. In Sec.
7
we construct the effective force coefficients with the cutoff
\nu\in(0,1]
and establish some of their properties. Let us stress that the bounds stated in this section are not uniform in the cutoff. In Sec.
8
we prove bounds for the cumulants of the effective force coefficients that are uniform in the cutoff
\nu\in(0,1]
. In Sec.
9
we show how to conclude convergence of the relevant local effective force coefficients as
\nu\searrow 0
using the bounds for cumulants. In Sec.
10
we construct the solution of the SPDE and express it as a function of the initial data, the relevant local effective force coefficients and the irrelevant force coefficients. Theorem
1.13
follows by combining the results of Sec.
9
and
10
. Appendix
List of symbols
contains the symbolic index.

2
Technique of the proof

In this section we give an informal description of the key steps of the proof of our result. We first give a general overview of the flow equation approach. Then we show how to solve the renormalization problem. Finally, we discuss the initial value problem.

2.1
Flow equation approach

Recall that
G
denotes the fractional heat kernel and we employ the notation
x=(\mathring{x},\bar{x})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}=\mathbb{M}
. The kernel
G(\mathring{x},\bar{x})
vanishes for
\mathring{x}<0
and is smooth outside the hyper-surface
\mathring{x}=0
(outside the point
(\mathring{x},\bar{x})=0
in the special case
\sigma\in 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
). Fix
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\mathrm{supp}\,\chi\subset(1,\infty)
and
\chi=1
on some neighborhood of
[2,\infty)
. The smooth kernel with UV cutoff
\mu\in(0,\infty)
is defined by
G_{\mu}(\mathring{x},\bar{x}):=\chi(\mathring{x}/\mu)G(\mathring{x},\bar{x})
. Note that
G_{\mu}(\mathring{x},\bar{x})=G(\mathring{x},\bar{x})
for
\mathring{x}\geq 2\mu
and
G_{\mu}(\mathring{x},\bar{x})=0
for
\mathring{x}\leq\mu
. We also set
G_{0}=G
. For any
T\in(0,\infty)
we have

G=-\int_{0}^{T}\dot{G}_{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu+G_{T},\qquad\dot{G}_{\mu}:=\partial_{\mu}G_{\mu}.

(2.1)

The following bound

\int_{\mathbb{M}}|x^{a}\partial^{b}\dot{G}_{\mu}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim[\mu]^{[a]-[b]}

(2.2)

holds uniformly in
\mu\in(0,1]
for all multi-indices
a,b\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
|\bar{a}|<\sigma
, where
[\mu]=\mu^{1/\sigma}
and
[a]=\sigma\mathring{a}+|\bar{a}|
for any
a=(\mathring{a},\bar{a})\in\mathfrak{M}=\mathbb{N}_{0}^{1+\mathsf{d}}
(see Def.
2.3
).

Let
\nu\in(0,1]
. Recall that the force
F_{\nu}[\varphi](x)
, defined by Eq. (
1.7
), is a local functional of
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{M})
. It is a sum of the noise
\varXi_{\nu}(x)
and some non-linearity
N_{\nu}[\varphi](x)
which is a polynomial in
\varphi(x)
and its derivatives of order strictly bounded by
\sigma
. Let
T\in(0,1]
and consider the following equation

\varPsi_{\nu}=(G-G_{T})\ast F_{\nu}[\varPsi_{\nu}]

(2.3)

in
\mathbb{M}
. This equation does not coincide with the mild form of the macroscopic equation (
1.6
). Nevertheless, the UV behaviors of solutions of both equations are similar. The basic object of the flow equation approach is the so-called effective force functional

C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})\ni\varphi\mapsto F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})

(2.4)

defined for
\mu\in[0,T]
. The effective force is generically a non-local functional of
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
. Under our assumptions about the noise
\varXi_{\nu}
the effective force
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi](x)
is a continuous function of
x\in\mathbb{M}
but it can be bounded uniformly in
\nu\in(0,1]
only in the space of distributions. By definition, the effective force satisfies the following flow equation

\langle\partial_{\mu}F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi],\psi\rangle=-\langle\mathrm{D}F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi,\dot{G}_{\mu}\ast F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]],\psi\rangle

(2.5)

with the boundary condition
F_{\nu,0}[\varphi]=F_{\nu}[\varphi]
, where
\varphi,\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
. The pairing between a distribution
V
and a test function
\psi
is denoted by
\langle V,\psi\rangle
and
\langle\mathrm{D}V[\varphi,\zeta],\psi\rangle
is the derivative of the functional
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})\ni\varphi\mapsto\langle V[\varphi],\psi\rangle\in\mathbb{R}
along
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
. Note that the boundary condition involves the force
F_{\nu}[\varphi]
, which is a local functional of
\varphi
.

Remark 2.1
.

Let
f_{\nu,T}:=F_{\nu,T}[0]
. For every
\mu\in[0,T]
we have

f_{\nu,T}=F_{\nu,\mu}[(G_{\mu}-G_{T})\ast f_{\nu,T}].

(2.6)

This identity follows from the fact that

g_{\nu,\mu}=f_{\nu,T}-F_{\nu,\mu}[(G_{\mu}-G_{T})\ast f_{\nu,T}]

(2.7)

satisfies the following linear ODE

\partial_{\mu}g_{\nu,\mu}=-\mathrm{D}F_{\nu,\mu}[(G_{\mu}-G_{T})\ast f_{\nu,T},\dot{G}_{\mu}\ast g_{\nu,\mu}]

(2.8)

with the trivial boundary condition
g_{\nu,T}=0
. Note that Eq. (
2.6
) with
\mu=0
implies that

\varPsi_{\nu}:=(G-G_{T})\ast F_{\nu,T}[0]

(2.9)

is a solution of Eq. (
2.3
).

Thus, in order to solve Eq. (
2.3
) for any
\nu\in(0,1]
it is enough to construct the effective force
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
. Similarly, to prove the existence of the limit
\nu\searrow 0
of the solution it is enough to investigate the limit
\nu\searrow 0
of the effective force
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
. In what follows, we assume that
T=1
and define the effective force
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
for
\nu\in(0,1]
,
\mu\in[0,1]
.

The starting point of the construction of the effective force is the following formal ansatz

\langle F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi],\psi\rangle:=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\lambda^{i}\,\langle F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu},\psi\otimes\varphi^{\otimes m}\rangle,

(2.10)

where
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
is the parameter that appears in Eq. (
1.7
) defining the force
F_{\nu}[\varphi]
. The distributions
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
,
i,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, are called the effective force coefficients. By definition
\langle F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu},\psi\otimes\varphi_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\varphi_{m}\rangle
is invariant under permutations of the test functions
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{m}\in\ C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
. The force
F_{\nu,0}[\varphi]=F_{\nu}[\varphi]
is defined by Eq. (
1.7
) in terms of the force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}
, satisfying the conditions specified in Assumption
3.11
. Hence, the force coefficients
F^{i,m}_{\nu,0}=F^{i,m}_{\nu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
are supported on the diagonal
\{(x,\ldots,x)\in\mathbb{M}^{1+m}\,|\,x\in\mathbb{M}\}
and only finitely many of them are not identically equal to zero. It also turns out that
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}=0
if
m>im_{\flat}
, where
m_{\flat}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
is such that all of the force coefficients
F^{i,m}_{\nu}
vanish identically whenever
m>m_{\flat}
. Moreover,
F^{0,0}_{\nu,\mu}
coincides with the noise
\varXi_{\nu}
. The flow equation (
2.5
) for
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
formally implies that the effective force coefficients
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
satisfy the following flow equation

\langle\partial_{\mu}^{\phantom{i}}F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}\,,\,\psi\otimes\varphi^{\otimes m}\rangle\\
=-\sum_{j=1}^{i}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\,(k+1)~\big\langle F^{j,k+1}_{\nu,\mu}\otimes F^{i-j,m-k}_{\nu,\mu},\psi\otimes\varphi^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathbf{V}\dot{G}_{\mu}\otimes\varphi^{\otimes(m-k)}\big\rangle

(2.11)

together with the boundary condition
F^{i,m}_{\nu,0}=F^{i,m}_{\nu}
, where
\mathbf{V}\dot{G}_{\mu}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M})
is defined by
\mathbf{V}\dot{G}_{\mu}(x,y):=\dot{G}_{\mu}(x-y)
.

The basic idea behind the flow equation approach is a recursive construction of the effective force coefficients
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
. We set
F^{0,0}_{\nu,\mu}=\varXi_{\nu}
and
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}\equiv 0
if
m>im_{\flat}
. Assuming that all
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
with
i<i_{\circ}
, or
i=i_{\circ}
and
m>m_{\circ}
have been constructed we define
\partial_{\mu}F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
with
i=i_{\circ}
and
m=m_{\circ}
with the use of the flow equation (
2.11
). Subsequently,
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
is defined by

F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}=F^{i,m}_{\nu}+\int_{0}^{\mu}\partial_{\eta}F^{i,m}_{\nu,\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\eta.

(2.12)

Using this procedure one can define all the effective force coefficients
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
for arbitrary
\nu\in(0,1]
,
\mu\in[0,1]
. It is also possible to prove the absolute convergence of the series on the RHS of Eq. (
2.10
) and construct the solution of Eq. (
2.3
) under the assumption that
\lambda
is sufficiently small and the noise
\varXi_{\nu}
is a periodic in space and time. However, this is not the aim of the present work. Instead of looking for global stationary solutions, we investigate the initial value problem. We give a non-perturbative construction of a solution for arbitrary
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
under the assumptions that the noise
\varXi_{\nu}
is periodic in space. Our analysis does not exclude a finite-time blow-up of the solution.

Remark 2.2
.

Let
\nu\in(0,1]
,
\mu\in[0,1]
. The effective force coefficients
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
can be alternatively defined recursively by the following equation

F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]=F_{\nu}[\varphi+(G-G_{\mu})\ast F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]],

(2.13)

where as above the effective force functional
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
coincides with the formal power series (
2.10
). More generally, for
\nu\in(0,1]
,
\eta,\mu\in[0,1]
it holds

F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]=F_{\nu,\eta}[\varphi+(G_{\eta}-G_{\mu})\ast F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]].

(2.14)

Note that Eq. (
2.13
) is a special case of Eq. (
2.14
) for
\eta=0
. To see that Eq. (
2.14
) follows from the flow equation (
2.11
) denote by
H_{\nu,\eta,\mu}[\varphi]
the difference between the LHS and RHS of Eq. (
2.14
). Then

\partial_{\mu}H_{\nu,\eta,\mu}[\varphi]=-\mathrm{D}H_{\nu,\eta,\mu}[\varphi,\dot{G}_{\mu}\ast F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]].

(2.15)

Using the above formula we prove by ascending induction on
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and descending induction on
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
that the coefficients
H_{\nu,\eta,\mu}^{i,m}
of the functional
H_{\nu,\eta,\mu}[\varphi]
defined by an analog of Eq. (
2.10
) vanish identically. We use the fact that
H_{\nu,\eta,\mu}^{i,m}=0
unless
m\leq im_{\flat}
. Let us mention that in certain situations one can make sense of Eq. (
2.14
) for
\eta\leq\mu
non-perturbatively.

2.2
Renormalization problem

Now let us describe the technique we use to bound the effective force coefficients
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
uniformly in
\nu,\mu\in(0,1]
and prove the existence of the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
. This step of the construction is very subtle and requires an appropriate fine-tuning of the coefficients
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}
, appearing in the expression (
1.2
) for the microscopic force
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{F}}}_{\nu}[\varphi]
(or equivalently, the coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}
, appearing in the expression (
1.7
) for the force
F_{\nu}[\varphi]
). This is the so-called renormalization problem.

Definition 2.3
.

For a spatial multi-index
a\in\bar{\mathfrak{M}}:=\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathsf{d}}
we set
|a|:=a^{1}+\ldots+a^{\mathsf{d}}
. For a spacetime multi-index
a\in\mathfrak{M}:=\mathbb{N}_{0}^{1+\mathsf{d}}
we set

|a|:=\mathring{a}+|\bar{a}|,\qquad[a]:=\sigma\mathring{a}+|\bar{a}|,\qquad a=(\mathring{a},\bar{a})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathsf{d}}.

(2.16)

We identify
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}
with a subset of
\mathfrak{M}
consisting of
a\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
\mathring{a}=0
. Let
\sigma_{\diamond}:=\sigma
if
\sigma\in 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
\sigma_{\diamond}:=\lceil\sigma\rceil-1
otherwise. By definition
\mathfrak{M}_{\sigma}
consists of
a\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
|a|\leq\sigma_{\diamond}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}_{\sigma}
consists of
a\in\bar{\mathfrak{M}}
such that
|a|<\sigma
.

Definition 2.4
.

For for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathfrak{M}^{m}
we set

|a|:=|a_{1}|+\ldots+|a_{m}|,\qquad[a]:=[a_{1}]+\ldots+[a_{m}].

(2.17)

We also set
\mathfrak{M}^{0}\equiv\{0\}
. The set
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
consists of all triples
(i,m,a)
such that
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\sigma}^{m}
. For
(i,m,a)\in\mathfrak{I}_{0}
we define

\varrho(i,m,a):=-\dim(\varXi)+m\,\dim(\varPhi)+i\,\dim(\lambda)+[a]\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.18)

Furthermore, we define

\mathfrak{I}^{-}_{0}:=\{(i,m,a)\in\mathfrak{I}_{0}\,|\,\varrho(i,m,a)\leq 0\},\quad\mathfrak{I}^{+}:=\{(i,m,a)\in\mathfrak{I}_{0}\,|\,\varrho(i,m,a)>0\}.

(2.19)

We also define
\mathfrak{I}:=\mathfrak{I}_{0}\setminus\{0,0,0\}
and
\mathfrak{I}^{-}:=\mathfrak{I}_{0}^{-}\setminus\{0,0,0\}
. The set
\mathfrak{I}
is called the set of indices and
\mathfrak{I}^{-}
and
\mathfrak{I}^{+}
are called the sets of relevant and irrelevant indices, respectively. The objects indexed by indices belonging
\mathfrak{I}^{-}
and
\mathfrak{I}^{+}
are called relevant and irrelevant, respectively.

Remark 2.5
.

Note that
\varrho(i,m,a)
introduced above generalizes
\varrho(i,m,a)
introduced in Def.
1.4
. Observe also that
\mathfrak{I}^{-}=\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}\subsetneq\mathfrak{I}^{+}
, where the sets
\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{\mp}
were introduced in Def.
1.4
.

Definition 2.6
.

We introduce the following notation:

•

i_{\diamond}
is the largest integer such that
\varrho(i_{\diamond},0,0)\leq 0
,

•

m_{\diamond}
is the largest integer such that
\varrho(1,m_{\diamond},0)\leq 0
,

•

i_{\triangleright}
is the largest integer such that
\varrho(i_{\triangleright},0,0)+\sigma\leq 0
,

•

i_{\flat}
is the smallest integer such that
i_{\flat}\geq i_{\diamond}
and
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}=0
for all
i>i_{\flat}
,

•

m_{\flat}
is the smallest integer such that
m_{\flat}\geq m_{\diamond}
and
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}=0
for all
m>m_{\flat}
,

•

i_{\dagger}:=i_{\flat}+i_{\triangleright}m_{\flat}
.

Remark 2.7
.

We have
i_{\diamond},m_{\diamond},i_{\flat},m_{\flat},i_{\dagger}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
i_{\triangleright}\in\{1,\ldots,i_{\diamond}\}
.

Definition 2.8
.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and a list of multi-indices
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathfrak{M}^{m}
the polynomial
\mathcal{X}^{m,a}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
is given by

\mathcal{X}^{m,a}(x;x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}):=(x-x_{1})^{a_{1}}\ldots(x-x_{m})^{a_{m}}.

(2.20)

The effective force coefficients
F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
and
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
are defined by Eq. (
1.18
). We have
F^{0,0}_{\nu,\mu}=F^{0,0,0}_{\nu,\mu}=f^{0,0,0}_{\nu,\mu}=\varXi_{\nu}
. We note that
F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
satisfies a certain flow equation that follows from the flow equation (
2.11
). For any sufficiently small
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
we prove inductively that

\displaystyle\|K_{\nu,\mu}^{m;{\mathtt{g}}}\ast F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}\|_{\mathcal{V}^{m}}\lesssim[\nu\vee\mu]^{\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a)},

(2.21)

\displaystyle\|K_{\nu,\mu}^{m;{\mathtt{g}}}\ast\partial_{\mu}F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}\|_{\mathcal{V}^{m}}\lesssim[\mu]^{\varepsilon-\sigma}\,[\nu\vee\mu]^{\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a)-\varepsilon},

(2.22)

uniformly in
\nu,\mu\in(0,1]
, where

\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a):=-\dim(\varXi)-\varepsilon+m\,(\dim(\varPhi)+\varepsilon)+i\,(\dim(\lambda)-\varepsilon m_{\flat})+[a]

(2.23)

and
[a]
denotes a degree of the list of multi-indices
a
. The above bounds control only the convolution of the effective force coefficients
F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
with some regularizing kernel
K_{\nu,\mu}^{m;{\mathtt{g}}}
with the characteristic length scale
[\nu\vee\mu]
. By assumption,
\dim(\lambda)>0
and
\dim(\varPhi)>0
. Hence, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
there are only finitely many
i,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a\in\mathfrak{M}^{m}
such that
\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a)<0
. If
i,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a\in\mathfrak{M}^{m}
are such that
\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a)<0
, then
F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
and
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
are called relevant. Otherwise,
F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
and
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
are called irrelevant. Let us also mention that given
i,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there always exists
\mathtt{l}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
such that
\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a)>0
for all
|a|=\mathtt{l}
.

Now we outline the proof of the bounds (
2.21
) and (
2.22
). We follow the recursive construction of the effective force coefficients presented in the previous section. We use the fact that
F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}=0
if
m>im_{\flat}
and assume the relevant coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
almost surely satisfy certain bounds of the form

\|K^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mu}\ast f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}\|\lesssim[\nu\vee\mu]^{\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a)}.

(2.24)

These bounds for the relevant coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
are proved using probabilistic arguments. We describe this step of the construction later. Assuming that the bound (
2.21
) holds for all
F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
such that
i<i_{\circ}
, or
i=i_{\circ}
and
m>m_{\circ}
we prove the bound (
2.22
) for
\partial_{\mu}F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
with
i=i_{\circ}
and
m=m_{\circ}
. To this end, we use a certain flow equation for
F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
. The bound for irrelevant
F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
follows then from the identity

F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}=F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,0}+\int_{0}^{\mu}\partial_{\eta}F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\eta.

(2.25)

Here we use the fact that

\int_{0}^{\mu}[\eta]^{\varepsilon-\sigma}\,[\nu\vee\eta]^{\rho-\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\lesssim[\nu]^{\rho}\vee[\mu]^{\rho},

(2.26)

for any
\rho\neq 0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
. Note that we obtain the correct bound for
F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
only if
\rho=\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a)>0
. To prove the bound in the case
\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a)<0
we use instead the following observation. Given
\mathtt{l}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
|a|<\mathtt{l}
there exists a map
\mathbf{X}_{\mathtt{l}}^{a}
with good continuity properties that takes
f^{i,m,b}_{\nu,\mu}
with
|b|<\mathtt{l}
and
F^{i,m,b}_{\nu,\mu}
with
|b|=\mathtt{l}
and returns
F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
. The existence of such a map follows from the Taylor theorem. We have

F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}=\mathbf{X}_{\mathtt{l}}^{a}(f^{i,m,b}_{\nu,\mu},F^{i,m,b}_{\nu,\mu}).

(2.27)

The parameter
\mathtt{l}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
is chosen in such a way that the arguments of the map
\mathbf{X}_{\mathtt{l}}^{a}
above involve only the effective force coefficients for which the bounds have already been proved. Finally we note that
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}=F^{i,m,0}_{\nu,\mu}
.

Using the technique described above one proves uniform bounds for the coefficients
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
, the convergence as
\nu\searrow 0
as well as the convergence of the series (
2.10
) for sufficiently small random
\lambda
. This allows to construct the solution of Eq. (
2.3
) and prove the existence of the limit
\nu\searrow 0
under the assumption that noise
\varXi_{\nu}
is spacetime periodic and
\lambda
is sufficiently small. The solution of the initial value problem for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, which is the aim of this work, requires additional analysis presented in the next section.

The crucial input necessary for the application of the above technique are uniform bounds for the relevant coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
. We prove such bounds using probability. Recall that
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
is defined in terms of
F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
. In order to bound
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
we first bound the cumulants of
\partial_{\mu}^{s}F^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
,
s\in\{0,1\}
, and subsequently use a Kolmogorov type argument. We also establish the existence of the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
. To this end, we use a diagonal argument adapted from
[
27
]
. We introduce a noise
\varXi_{\kappa;\nu}
obtained by convolving
\varXi_{\nu}
with some regularizing kernel at scale
\kappa\in(0,1]
. Next, we construct the force
F_{\kappa;\nu}[\varphi]
and the effective force
F_{\kappa;\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
as well as the coefficients
F^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,\mu}
and
f^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,\mu}
corresponding to the regularized noise
\varXi_{\kappa;\nu}
. The existence of the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}f^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,\mu}
follows from the existence of the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varXi_{\kappa;\nu}
for any
\kappa\in(0,1]
and uniform bounds for cumulants of
\partial^{s}_{\mu}\partial^{r}_{\kappa}F^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,\mu}
,
s,r\in\{0,1\}
.

The proof of the bounds for the cumulants of the effective force coefficients is based on a certain flow equation for the cumulants that follows from the flow equation (
2.11
). As in the case of the effective force coefficients the cumulants can be divided into the relevant and irrelevant ones. The bounds for the irrelevant cumulants are proved inductively by integrating the flow equation with the boundary condition at
\mu=0
. The only relevant cumulants are the expected values of the relevant coefficients
F^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,\mu}
. In order to bound such expectations we first bound the expectations of the relevant
f^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,\mu}
. Subsequently, we use the Taylor theorem and the bounds for the irrelevant cumulants. More specifically, we prove the following uniform bounds

|\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\partial_{\mu}^{s}\partial_{\kappa}^{r}f^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,\mu}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}|\lesssim[\kappa]^{(\varepsilon-\sigma)r}[\mu]^{(\varepsilon-\sigma)s}[\kappa\vee\nu\vee\mu]^{\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a)-\varepsilon s}

(2.28)

where
s,r\in\{0,1\}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
is sufficiently small and
\kappa,\nu,\mu\in(0,1]
. Note that the expected value of
\partial_{\mu}^{s}\partial_{\kappa}^{r}f^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,\mu}(x)
does not depend on
x\in\mathbb{M}
by the assumed stationarity of the noise
\varXi_{\nu}
. The above bound with
s=1
follows from the induction hypothesis and the flow equation. In order to prove the bound for
s=0
and
\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a)<0
we use the identity

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\partial_{\kappa}^{r}f^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,\mu}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\partial_{\kappa}^{r}f^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}-\int_{\mu}^{1}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\partial_{\eta}\partial_{\kappa}^{r}f^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,\eta}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta.

(2.29)

with the following boundary condition

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{\nu},\qquad\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\partial_{\kappa}f^{i,m,a}_{\kappa;\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=0.

(2.30)

Note that for
\kappa=0
the above boundary condition coincides with the renormalization condition expressed in Assumption
3.11
. The bound (
2.28
) with
s=0
follows now from the following estimate

\int_{\mu}^{1}[\mu]^{\varepsilon-\sigma}\,[\nu\vee\mu]^{\rho-\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\lesssim 1\vee[\nu\vee\mu]^{\rho}

(2.31)

valid for any
\rho\neq 0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and the fact that
1\vee[\nu\vee\mu]^{\rho}\leq[\nu\vee\mu]^{\rho}
for
\rho<0
and
\nu,\mu\in(0,1]
. Finally, we note that using Eq. (
2.29
) with
r=0
,
\kappa=0
and
\mu=0
one can compute the relevant force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu\phantom{0}}=f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,0}=f^{i,m,a}_{0;\nu,0}
. The above choice of the values of these coefficients guarantees the existence of the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
for any
\mu\in(0,1]
. We also obtain
|f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}|\lesssim[\nu]^{\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a)}
. Since for relevant
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
we have
\varrho_{\varepsilon}(i,m,a)<0
the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
need not exist. We stress that the values of the relevant coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
for any
\nu\in(0,1]
are uniquely determined by the flow equation and the renormalization conditions expressed in terms of the effective force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,\mu}
with
\mu=1
.

Remark 2.9
.

In the approach based on the theory of regularity structures the renormalization problem is tackled at the level of the stochastic estimates of a model and a very general solution of this problem (applicable to sub-critical singular SPDEs with local differential operators) was given in
[
8
]
. Recall that a model consists of certain multi-linear functionals of the noise that can be represented by tree diagrams. The stochastic estimates of a model involve expectations of products of trees and are represented by diagrams with loops. The approach to the renormalization problem taken in
[
8
]
exploits this diagrammatical representation. One of the main obstacles in this approach is the problem of overlapping divergences which appears when a given diagram have two or more divergent sub-diagrams which are neither nested nor disjoint. The solution of above problem given in
[
8
]
uses the ideas from the BPHZ renormalization technique in perturbative QFT and involves a decomposition of the integration domain into the Hepp sectors and the application of a variant of the Zimmerman forest formula.

In contrast, in the flow equation approach the solution of the renormalization problem does not rely on any diagrammatical representation. As explained above, we construct the effective force coefficients recursively using the flow equation. To prove bounds for the cumulants of the effective force coefficients we impose appropriate boundary conditions when solving the flow equation. In particular, the boundary conditions for the expectations of the relevant effective force coefficients coincide with the BPHZ renormalization conditions. The correct values of the counterterms are determined automatically by the flow equation and the boundary conditions. The problem of overlapping divergences does not exist in our approach. Note that the decomposition into different spatial scales (like the Hepp sectors) needed to solve the problem of overlapping divergences in the BPHZ approach is built into the foundation of the flow equation approach. Let us also mention that our technique does not require any positive renormalization corresponding to a recentering procedure. In particular, we do not need to define a structure group.

2.3
Initial value problem

In this section we show how to use the ideas described in the previous two sections to solve the initial value problem for the parabolic equation (
1.6
). Even though this equation is not of the form (
2.3
), as we will argue below, the renormalization problem for the mild form of this equation is the same as the renormalization problem for Eq. (
2.3
), which was discussed in the previous section.

In what follows, the Dirac delta at
t\in\mathbb{R}
is denoted by
\delta_{t}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
and the characteristic function of an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is denoted by
1_{I}
. We also set
(\mathtt{w}f)(\mathring{x},\bar{x})=\mathtt{w}(\mathring{x})f(\mathring{x},\bar{x})
for any
\mathtt{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{M})
. Let
\nu\in(0,1]
. We would like to construct the solution of the following equation

\varPhi_{\nu}=G\ast(1_{(0,\infty)}F_{\nu}[\varPhi_{\nu}]+\delta_{0}\otimes\phi_{\nu})

(2.32)

and study its limit
\nu\searrow 0
. Because of the singularity of
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varXi_{\nu}
and the necessity to renormalize the equation the solution theory developed in this paper requires that in the limit
\nu\searrow 0
the initial condition
\phi_{\nu}
is a small perturbation (in the sense specified below) of the initial condition that would lead to a stationary solution. We note that a similar assumption was made in
[
4
]
. More specifically, we suppose that the initial data
\phi_{\nu}
is of the following form
\phi_{\nu}=\phi^{\triangleright}_{\nu}+\phi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}
.

The process
\phi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}
is given by
\phi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}:=\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}(0,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})
, where

\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}:=(G-G_{1})\ast f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}=G\ast(f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}-\check{f}^{\triangleright}_{\nu}),\qquad f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}:=\sum_{i=0}^{i_{\triangleright}}F^{i,0}_{\nu,1},\qquad\check{f}^{\triangleright}_{\nu}:=\mathbf{Q}G_{1}\ast f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}

(2.33)

and the effective force coefficients
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
were constructed in the previous section. By definition
i_{\triangleright}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is the largest integer such that
\dim(\varPhi)\geq i_{\triangleright}\dim(\lambda)
. We show that
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}
exists in the space of distributions. Since the kernel
\mathbf{Q}G_{1}
is smooth the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\check{f}^{\triangleright}_{\nu}
is a smooth function. Moreover, the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}=\varPhi^{\triangleright}_{0}
exists in the parabolic Besov space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H})
for every
\alpha<-\dim(\varPhi)
.

Definition 2.10
.

The dimension of the regular part of the initial data, denoted by
\dim(\phi)
, is fixed such that
0<\dim(\phi)<\dim(\lambda)
and if
\dim(\varPhi)<\dim(\lambda)
, then
\dim(\phi)=\dim(\varPhi)
.

Remark 2.11
.

This dimension need not have any physical interpretation.

The process
\phi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}
is such that the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\phi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}=\phi_{0}^{\mathrm{reg}}
exists in the Besov space
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T})
for every
\beta<-\dim(\phi)
. We note that if
\dim(\lambda)>\dim(\varPhi)
, that is Eq. (
2.32
) is far from criticality, then
\dim(\phi)=\dim(\varPhi)
and the initial data
\phi_{0}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is allowed to have the same regularity as the expected regularity of the solution. If
\dim(\lambda)\leq\dim(\varPhi)
, then
0<\dim(\phi)<\dim(\lambda)
. Consequently, if
\dim(\lambda)
is small, that is Eq. (
2.32
) is close to criticality, then the regularity of
\phi_{0}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is allowed to be only slightly negative.

In order to take advantage of our assumption about the initial data we introduce the following ansatz

\varPhi_{\nu}=\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}+\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}

(2.34)

and show that Eq. (
2.32
) is equivalent to a certain equation for
\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}
with initial data coinciding with
\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. To this end, let us first note that the equality
\phi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}=(G\ast(f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}-\check{f}^{\triangleright}_{\nu}))(0,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})
and the semi-group property of the fractional heat kernel imply that

G\ast(\delta_{0}\otimes\phi_{\nu}^{\triangleright})=G\ast 1_{(-\infty,0]}(f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}-\check{f}^{\triangleright}_{\nu})

(2.35)

in the region
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}\subset\mathbb{M}
. Consequently, in that region region Eq. (
2.32
) is equivalent to the equation

\varPhi_{\nu}=G\ast(1_{(0,\infty)}F_{\nu}[\varPhi_{\nu}]+1_{(-\infty,0]}(f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}-\check{f}^{\triangleright}_{\nu})+\delta_{0}\otimes\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}).

(2.36)

It is easy to see that
\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}=\varPhi_{\nu}-\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}
satisfies the following equation

\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}=G\ast(1_{(0,\infty)}(F_{\nu}[\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}+\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}]-f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}+\check{f}^{\triangleright}_{\nu})+\delta_{0}\otimes\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}).

(2.37)

Our goal is to prove that the classical maximal solution
\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}
of Eq. (
2.37
) converges to some distribution
\breve{\varPhi}_{0}
as
\nu\searrow 0
.

Remark 2.12
.

The decomposition (
2.34
) is a higher order variant of the so-called Da Prato-Debussche trick
[
13
]
. Here its purpose is to deal with the effect of the initial condition. A similar idea was used in
[
4
]
.

The solution of Eq. (
2.37
) up to a possible explosion time can be constructed by patching together solutions of the following equations

\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}=(G-G_{T})\ast(1_{(t,t+6i_{\dagger})}(F_{\nu}[\breve{\varPhi}+\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}]-f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}+\check{f}^{\triangleright}_{\nu})+\delta_{t}\otimes\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}})

(2.38)

posed in the domains of the form
[t,t+T]\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}\subset\mathbb{M}
for sufficiently small
T\in(0,1)
and appropriate initial data
\phi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}
at time
t\in[0,\infty)
. Recall that
\mathrm{supp}\,G\subset[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
,
\mathrm{supp}\,G_{T}\subset(T,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
and the force
F_{\nu}[\varphi]
is a local functional that involves only spatial derivatives of
\varphi
. We stress that by abusing the notation we now set
\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}=\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}(t,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})
. We also note that one could replace the time interval
(t,t+6i_{\dagger})
with any time interval containing
(t,t+1)
. It is convenient to choose
i_{\dagger}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
as in Def.
2.6
. Since
\delta_{t}\otimes\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
is not a function of time we introduce another ansatz

\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}=\tilde{\varPhi}_{\nu}+\varPhi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}},

(2.39)

where

\varPhi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}:=(G-G_{1})\ast f_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}},\qquad f_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}:=\delta_{t}\otimes\phi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}.

(2.40)

One shows that in the region
[t,t+T]\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}\subset\mathbb{M}
the function
\varPhi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}
coincides with
(G-G_{T})\ast f_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}
and Eq. (
2.38
) is equivalent to the following equation

\tilde{\varPhi}_{\nu}=(G-G_{T})\ast 1_{(t,t+6i_{\dagger})}(F_{\nu}[\tilde{\varPhi}_{\nu}+\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}+\varPhi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}]-f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}+\check{f}^{\triangleright}_{\nu}).

(2.41)

We rewrite the above equation in the form

\tilde{\varPhi}_{\nu}=(G-G_{T})\ast\tilde{F}_{\nu}[\tilde{\varPhi}_{\nu}]

(2.42)

upon introducing the notation

\begin{gathered}\tilde{F}_{\nu}[\varphi]:=1_{(t,t+6i_{\dagger})}\check{F}_{\nu}[\varphi]=1_{(t,t+6i_{\dagger})}(\hat{F}_{\nu}[\varphi+\varPhi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}]+\check{f}_{\nu}^{\triangleright}),\\
\check{F}_{\nu}[\varphi]:=\hat{F}_{\nu}[\varphi+\varPhi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}]-f_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}+\check{f}_{\nu}^{\triangleright},\qquad\hat{F}_{\nu}[\varphi]:=F_{\nu}[\varphi+\varPhi^{\triangleright}_{\nu}]-f_{\nu}^{\triangleright}.\end{gathered}

(2.43)

In order to avoid confusion observe that the measure
1_{(t,t+6i_{\dagger})}\delta_{t}
vanishes identically. Hence,
1_{(t,t+6i_{\dagger})}f_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}=0
.

In order to solve Eq. (
2.41
) in the domain
[t,t+T]\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
with sufficiently small
T\in(0,1)
we construct the effective force

\langle\tilde{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi],\psi\rangle:=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\lambda^{i}\,\langle\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu},\psi\otimes\varphi^{\otimes m}\rangle

(2.44)

for
\nu\in(0,1]
,
\mu\in(0,T]
. By definition
\tilde{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
satisfies the flow equation (
2.5
) together with the initial condition
\tilde{F}_{\nu,0}[\varphi]=\tilde{F}_{\nu}[\varphi]
. Using the technique described in Sec.
2.1
one shows that

\tilde{\varPhi}_{\nu}:=(G-G_{T})\ast\tilde{F}_{\nu,T}[0]=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\lambda^{i}\,(G-G_{T})\ast\tilde{F}^{i,0}_{\nu,T}

(2.45)

solves Eq. (
2.41
). The effective force coefficients
\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
are defined recursively as outlined in Sec.
2.1
and satisfy the flow equation (
2.11
). In order to prove uniform bounds for
\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
and show the existence of the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
we use the technique presented below. The solution of Eq. (
2.37
) in the domain
[t,t+T]\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
coincides with

\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}=\varPhi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}+\tilde{\varPhi}_{\nu},\qquad\varPhi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}=(G-G_{1})\ast(\delta_{t}\otimes\phi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}).

(2.46)

The maximal solution of Eq. (
2.37
) is constructed by patching together solutions in time intervals
[t,t+T]
.

Finally, let us describe the technique we use to prove uniform bounds for
\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
and show the existence of the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
. We first study the effective force
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
for the equation
\varPsi_{\nu}=(G-G_{1})\ast F_{\nu}[\varPsi_{\nu}]
. The above equation coincides with Eq. (
2.3
) with
T=1
and the effective force coefficients
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
are constructed and bounded uniformly in
\nu\in(0,1]
,
\mu\in[0,1]
as described in Sec.
2.2
. This step requires the renormalization of the relevant coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
of the force
F_{\nu}[\varphi]
. Note that the effective force coefficients
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
do not involve the initial condition. We call
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
the effective force at stationarity and
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
the effective force coefficients at stationarity.

Remark 2.13
.

We shall use below the following observation. Assume that a functional
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
satisfies the flow equation (
2.5
), then for any
f_{\nu}
and
\varPsi_{\nu}
the functional
\tilde{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]=F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi+(G_{\mu}-G_{1})\ast f_{\nu}+\varPsi_{\nu}]-f_{\nu}
also satisfies the flow equation (
2.5
).

We introduce a new effective force
\hat{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
by the following relation between formal power series

\hat{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]:=F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi+(G_{\mu}-G_{1})\ast f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}]-f_{\nu}^{\triangleright},

(2.47)

where
f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}=\sum_{i=0}^{i_{\triangleright}}F^{i,0}_{\nu,1}
. The effective force
\hat{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
satisfies the flow equation (
2.5
) with the boundary condition
\hat{F}_{\nu,0}[\varphi]=\hat{F}_{\nu}[\varphi]
, where
\hat{F}_{\nu}[\varphi]
was introduced in Eq. (
2.43
). Subsequently, we define another effective force formally given by

\check{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]=\hat{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi+(G_{\mu}-G_{1})\ast f_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}+(G-G_{\mu})\ast\check{f}_{\nu}^{\triangleright}]-f_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}+\check{f}_{\nu}^{\triangleright},

(2.48)

where
\check{f}^{\triangleright}_{\nu}=\mathbf{Q}G_{1}\ast f^{\triangleright}_{\nu}
and
f^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}=\delta_{t}\otimes\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. The effective force
\check{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
satisfies the flow equation (
2.5
) with the boundary condition
\check{F}_{\nu,0}[\varphi]=\check{F}_{\nu}[\varphi]
, where
\check{F}_{\nu}[\varphi]
was introduced in Eq. (
2.43
).

Recall that we would like to construct and bound the effective force
\tilde{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
. Using the flow equation (
2.11
) one proves that

\langle\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu},\psi\otimes\varphi^{\otimes m}\rangle=\langle\check{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu},\psi\otimes\varphi^{\otimes m}\rangle

(2.49)

for any
\psi,\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
such that
\mathrm{supp}\,\psi\subset(t+2i\mu,t+6i_{\dagger})\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
. The above formula plays a crucial role in the construction since it provides a relation between the effective forces
\tilde{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
and
\check{F}_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
. Note that Eq. (
2.49
) holds true for
\mu=0
by Eq. (
2.43
). In order to prove Eq. (
2.49
) for
\mu\in(0,1]
one uses a simple inductive argument based on the following support property
\mathrm{supp}\,\dot{G}_{\mu}\subset[0,2\mu]\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
of the scale decomposition of the fractional heat kernel.

The construction of the effective force
\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
in a small neighborhood of the complement of the domain
(t+2i\mu,t+6i_{\dagger})\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}\times\mathbb{M}^{m}
does not involve renormalization. Here we crucially use the fact that the coefficients
\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
are not too singular thanks to the Da Prato-Debussche trick,
\tilde{F}^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
is supported in the domain
[t,t+6i_{\dagger}]\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}\times\mathbb{M}^{m}
and the Lebesgue measure of
[t,t+6i_{\dagger}]\setminus(t+2i\mu,t+6i_{\dagger})
is proportional to
\mu=[\mu]^{\sigma}
.

3
Statement of the result

In this section we list the standing assumptions of the paper and give the precise statement of the main result. We present several concrete examples to which our result applies. We also discuss related results known in the literature.

3.1
Assumptions

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\bar{\mathbb{M}}=\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
, where
\mathsf{d}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
is called the spatial dimension. We call
\mathbb{M}:=\mathbb{R}\times\bar{\mathbb{M}}
the spacetime. We denote points in
\mathbb{M}
by
x=(\mathring{x},\bar{x})
, where
\bar{x}=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{\mathsf{d}})\in\bar{\mathbb{M}}
. The coordinate
\mathring{x}\in\mathbb{R}
is called the time. For
\nu\in(0,1]
let
\mathbb{S}(\nu)=\mathbb{R}/(2\pi/[\nu]\,\mathbb{Z})
be the circle of size
[\nu]^{-1}=\nu^{-1/\sigma}
, let
\mathbb{T}(\nu):=\mathbb{S}(\nu)^{\mathsf{d}}
be the
\mathsf{d}
-dimensional torus of size
[\nu]^{-1}
and let
\mathbb{H}(\nu):=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}(\nu)
. We also set
\mathbb{T}(0)=\bar{\mathbb{M}}
and
\mathbb{H}(0)=\mathbb{M}
. The absolute value of
t\in\mathbb{R}
is denoted by
|t|
. For
\bar{x}=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{\mathsf{d}})\in\bar{\mathbb{M}}
we set

|\bar{x}|_{\bar{\mathbb{M}}}\equiv|\bar{x}|:=\max_{k\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{d}\}}|x^{k}|,\qquad|\bar{x}|_{\mathbb{T}(\nu)}:=\min_{\bar{y}\in(2\pi/[\nu]\,\mathbb{Z})^{\mathsf{d}}}|\bar{x}+\bar{y}|_{\bar{\mathbb{M}}},\quad\nu\in(0,1].

(3.1)

For
x=(\mathring{x},\bar{x})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}=\mathbb{M}
we set

|x|_{\mathbb{M}}:=[\mathring{x}]\vee|\bar{x}|_{\bar{\mathbb{M}}},\qquad|x|_{\mathbb{H}(\nu)}:=[\mathring{x}]\vee|\bar{x}|_{\mathbb{T}(\nu)},~~\nu\in(0,1],\qquad[\mathring{x}]:=|\mathring{x}|^{1/\sigma}.

(3.2)

We omit
\nu
in the notation if
\nu=1
. Functions
\mathbb{H}(\nu)\to\mathbb{R}
are always identified with periodic in space functions
\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Assumption 3.2
.

The family of random fields
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
,
\nu\in[0,1]
, defined on the spacetime
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
satisfies the following conditions.

(A)

For any
\nu\in[0,1]
the field
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}(\nu))
is stationary and centered. In particular, if
\nu\in(0,1]
, then
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
is periodic in space with period
2\pi/[\nu]
.

(B)

It holds

\int_{\mathbb{M}}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{0}(0)\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{0}(x)\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\,\mathrm{d}x=1,\qquad\sup_{\nu\in[0,1]}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}|\partial^{a}\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}(0)|^{n}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}<\infty

(3.3)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and multi-indices
a\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
|a|\leq 3
.

(C)

For any
\nu\in[0,1]
and any compact sets
\mathcal{O}_{1}
,
\mathcal{O}_{2}\subset\mathbb{H}(\nu)
such that

\inf_{x_{1}\in\mathcal{O}_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{O}_{2}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|_{\mathbb{H}(\nu)}\geq 1

(3.4)

the
\sigma
-algebras
generated by
\{\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}(x)\,|\,x\in\mathcal{O}_{1}\}
and
\{\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}(x)\,|\,x\in\mathcal{O}_{2}\}
are independent.

(D)

For every
\nu\in(0,1]
, there is a coupling of
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{0}
and
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
such that for every
T>0
and
R\in(0,\pi)
it holds

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathring{x}|\leq T/\nu\\
|\bar{x}|\leq R/[\nu]\end{subarray}}[\nu]^{-2\dim(\varXi)}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}|\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{0}(\mathring{x},\bar{x})-\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}(\mathring{x},\bar{x})|^{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=0\,.

(3.5)

Remark 3.3
.

The assumption stated above is a trivial adaptation of the assumption formulated in
[
27
]
.

Remark 3.4
.

Note that for any
\nu\in(0,1]
the field
\varXi_{\nu}
obtained by rescaling
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
as in Eq. (
1.8
) is periodic in space with period
2\pi
and
\varXi_{\nu}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{H})
. Recall that
\varXi_{0}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H})
denotes the white noise on
\mathbb{H}
. There is no relation between the fields
\varXi_{0}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H})
and
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{M})
.

Remark 3.5
.

Let
\xi_{0}
to be the white noise on
\mathbb{M}
and for
\nu\in(0,1]
let
\xi_{\nu}
be the periodic extension of
\xi_{0}
restricted to
\mathbb{R}\times[-\pi/[\nu],\pi/[\nu])^{\mathsf{d}}\subset\mathbb{M}
. It is easy to see that for any function
M\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
supported in the ball of radius
1/2
centered at the origin such that
\int_{\mathbb{M}}M(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=1
the family of random fields
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}=M\ast\xi_{\nu}
,
\nu\in[0,1]
, satisfies the above assumption. Note that for any
\nu\in(0,1]
the law of the field
\varXi_{\nu}
obtained by rescaling
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
coincides with the law of the field
\varXi_{\nu}=M_{\nu}\ast\varXi_{0}
, where
\varXi_{0}
is the white noise on
\mathbb{H}
and
M_{\nu}(\mathring{x},\bar{x}):=[\nu]^{-\mathsf{D}}M(\mathring{x}/\nu,\bar{x}/[\nu])
,
\mathsf{D}=\sigma+\mathsf{d}
.

Remark 3.6
.

A non-trivial example of a family of random fields
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
,
\nu\in[0,1]
, satisfying the above assumption is obtained as follows
[
27
]
. Let
\pi_{0}
be the homogeneous Poisson process on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{M}
and for
\nu\in(0,1]
let
\pi_{\nu}
be the periodic extension of
\pi_{0}
restricted to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times[-\pi/[\nu],\pi/[\nu])^{\mathsf{d}}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{M}
. For any functions
M\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{M})
supported in the ball of radius
1/2
centered at the origin such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{M}}M(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x=0
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}}M(s,x)M(s,y)\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=1
the family of random fields
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{M}}M(s,x-y)\,\pi_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}y)
,
\nu\in[0,1]
, satisfies the above assumption.

Remark 3.7
.

By the above assumption for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
a\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
|a|\leq 3
the bound

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{H}}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}|\partial^{a}\varXi_{\nu}(x)|^{n}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\lesssim[\nu]^{-n\dim(\varXi)-n[a]}

(3.6)

holds uniformly in
\nu\in(0,1]
. Moreover, as proved in
[
27
, Sec. 6]
, it holds
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varXi_{\nu}=\varXi_{0}
in distribution in the parabolic Besov space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H})
for any
\alpha<-\dim(\varXi)
, where
\varXi_{0}
is the white noise on
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
.

Assumption 3.8
.

The random fields
\phi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}
,
\nu\in(0,1]
, defined on
\mathbb{T}
are smooth. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and any
a\in\bar{\mathfrak{M}}
such that
|a|\leq 2\lfloor\sigma/2\rfloor+4
the bound

\sup_{\bar{x}\in\mathbb{T}}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}|\partial^{a}\phi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}(\bar{x})|^{n}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\lesssim[\nu]^{-n\dim(\phi)-n|a|}

(3.7)

holds uniformly in
\nu\in(0,1]
. Moreover, there exists a random distribution
\phi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T})
such that
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\phi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}=\phi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{0}
in law in the Besov space
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T})
for any
\beta<-\dim(\phi)
.

Remark 3.9
.

If the random fields
\varXi_{\nu}
and
\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}
are not independent, then we require in addition that

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,(\varXi_{\nu},\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}})=(\varXi_{0},\phi_{0}^{\mathrm{reg}})

(3.8)

in law in
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T})
for any
\alpha<-\dim(\varXi)
and any
\beta<-\dim(\phi)
.

Remark 3.10
.
