Since it is reasonable to expect that both the noise and the initial condition are smooth at the microscopic scale no effort was made to state optimal assumptions regarding differentiability.

Assumption 3.11
.

The force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}=f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,0}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}
, satisfy the following conditions. We assume that for the irrelevant coefficients the following bounds

|f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,0}|\lesssim[\nu]^{\varrho(i,m,a)-\varepsilon},\qquad(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},

(3.9)

hold uniformly in
\nu\in(0,1]
. Moreover, we demand that there exist
i_{\flat},m_{\flat}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
such that
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,0}
vanishes unless
i\in\{0,\ldots,i_{\flat}\}
,
m\in\{0,\ldots,m_{\flat}\}
and
a\in\bar{\mathfrak{M}}^{m}
is such that
|a|<\sigma
. Furthermore, we suppose that there exists a family of continuous functions
[0,1]\ni\nu\mapsto\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
, such that it holds

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{\nu},\qquad(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-},

(3.10)

where the relevant coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,1}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
, are defined in terms of the force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}
, as explained in the introduction.

Remark 3.12
.

Note that the above assumption implies that
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{\nu}=\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{0}
and
|\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{\nu}|\lesssim 1
uniformly in
\nu\in[0,1]
. Moreover, as we will see the bound (
3.9
) actually holds for all
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}
.

Assumption 3.13
.

If
\sigma\notin 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
, then we assume that
\lceil\sigma\rceil-1>\sigma-2\dim(\lambda)
.

Remark 3.14
.

It plays an important role in our construction that the function

\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}\ni\bar{x}\mapsto x^{a}G(x)\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\quad x\equiv(\mathring{x},\bar{x}),

(3.11)

is absolutely integrable for every
\mathring{x}\in(0,\infty)
and every
a\in\mathfrak{M}_{\sigma}
, where
G
is the fractional heat kernel. In general, our construction requires that the above function is absolutely integrable for all
a\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
|a|\leq\lfloor\sigma-2\dim(\lambda)\rfloor+1
. However, if
\sigma\notin 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
, because of the long-range character of the fractional heat kernel the function (
3.11
) is integrable only for
a=(\mathring{a},\bar{a})\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
|\bar{a}|<\sigma
. Under Assumption
3.13
our construction uses only the functions (
3.11
) with
a\in\mathfrak{M}_{\sigma}
which are absolutely integrable.

3.2
Main theorem

Consider the mild formulation of the macroscopic equation (
1.6
)

\varPhi_{\nu}=G\ast(1_{(0,\infty)}F_{\nu}[\varPhi_{\nu}]+\delta_{0}\otimes\phi_{\nu}),

(3.12)

where
\ast
denotes the space-time convolution,
1_{(0,\infty)}F_{\nu}[\varphi](x):=1_{(0,\infty)}(\mathring{x})F_{\nu}[\varphi](x)
,
\delta_{0}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
is the Dirac delta distribution at
\mathring{x}=0
, the initial data is of the form
\phi_{\nu}=\phi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}+\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}
and
G(\mathring{x},{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
,
\mathring{x}\in(0,\infty)
, is the heat kernel of the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\sigma/2}
extended for
\mathring{x}\in(-\infty,0]
in such a way that
G(0,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})=\delta_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
and
G(\mathring{x},{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})=0
for
\mathring{x}\in(-\infty,0)
. Recall that the force
F_{\nu}[\varphi]
is defined by Eq. (
1.7
) in terms of the noise
\varXi_{\nu}
, the force coefficients
f^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}
, and the parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that
G\ast(\delta_{0}\otimes\phi_{\nu})
is the unique solution of the equation
\mathbf{Q}\varPhi_{\nu}=0
in the domain
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
with the initial condition
\varPhi_{\nu}(0,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})=\phi_{\nu}
. We also remind the reader that the stochastic process
\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}
introduced in Eq. (
1.21
) coincides with the stationary solution
\varPsi_{\nu}
of the equation
\varPsi_{\nu}=(G-G_{1})\ast F_{\nu}[\varPsi_{\nu}]
truncated at order
\lambda^{i_{\triangleright}}
, where
i_{\triangleright}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is chosen in such that
\varPsi_{\nu}-\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}
,
\nu\in(0,1]
, is uniformly bounded (as a formal power series in
\lambda
) in some space with positive regularity. Moreover, by definition
\phi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}:=\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}(0,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})
.

Remark 3.15
.

In Sec.
2.3
, using the fact that the force
F_{\nu}[\varphi]
is a local functional that involves only spatial derivatives of
\varphi
, we showed that if
\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}
satisfies the equation

\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}=G\ast(1_{(0,\infty)}(F_{\nu}[\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}+\varPhi^{\triangleright}_{\nu}]-\mathbf{Q}\varPhi^{\triangleright}_{\nu})+\delta_{0}\otimes\phi^{\mathrm{reg}}_{\nu}),

(3.13)

then
\varPhi_{\nu}=\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}+\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}
satisfies Eq. (
3.12
).

Definition 3.16
.

For
\varepsilon>0
let
\alpha\equiv\alpha_{\varepsilon}:=-\dim(\varPhi)-\varepsilon
,
\beta\equiv\beta_{\varepsilon}:=-\dim(\phi)-\varepsilon
and
\gamma\equiv\gamma_{\varepsilon}:=\sigma-\varepsilon
.

Theorem 3.17
.

Suppose that the assumptions formulated in the previous section hold true. Let
\varepsilon>0
be sufficiently small. There exist:

•

a coupling between
(\varXi_{\nu},\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}})
,
\nu\in[0,1]
,

•

a family of random stopping times
\breve{T}_{\nu}\in[0,\infty]
,
\nu\in[0,1]
,

•

a family of stochastic processes
\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}\in C([0,\breve{T}_{\nu}),\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}))
,
\nu\in(0,1]
,

•

a stationary stochastic process
\varPhi_{0}^{\triangleright}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
,

•

a stochastic process
\breve{\varPhi}_{0}\in C([0,\breve{T}_{0}),\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}))

such that:

(A)

for every
\nu\in(0,1]
the stochastic process
\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}\in C(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}))
is well-defined and stationary, it holds
\lim_{t\nearrow\breve{T}_{\nu}}\|\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}(t,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}=\infty
on the event
\{\breve{T}_{\nu}<\infty\}
and the stochastic process
\varPhi_{\nu}=\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}+\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}\in C([0,\breve{T}_{\nu}),\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}))
is the unique pathwise solution of Eq. (
3.12
) with
\phi_{\nu}=\phi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}+\phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{reg}}
,

(B)

the law of the stochastic process
\varPhi^{\triangleright}_{0}
depends only on the choice of the renormalization constants
\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
,

(C)

\varPhi_{0}^{\triangleright}=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\nu}^{\triangleright}
in the space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
in probability,

(D)

\lim_{t\nearrow\breve{T}_{0}}\|\breve{\varPhi}_{0}(t,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T})}=\infty
on the event
\{\breve{T}_{0}<\infty\}
and the law of the stochastic process
\breve{\varPhi}_{0}
depends only on the initial data
\phi_{0}^{\mathrm{reg}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T})
and the choice of the renormalization constants
\mathfrak{f}^{i,m,a}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
,

(E)

for any
T\in(0,\infty)
on the event
\{T<\breve{T}_{0}\}
it holds
T<\breve{T}_{\nu}
for all sufficiently small
\nu\in(0,1]
and
\breve{\varPhi}_{0}=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\breve{\varPhi}_{\nu}
in the space
C([0,T],\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}))
in probability.

Remark 3.18
.

For the definitions of the functions spaces appearing in the above statement see Sec.
5
. The space
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T})
coincides with (some separable subspace of) the standard Besov space
\mathcal{B}_{\infty,\infty}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T})
. The space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})\equiv\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H})
locally coincides with (a separable subspace of) the parabolic Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
. The space
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T})
is contained in
C^{\lfloor\gamma\rfloor}(\mathbb{T})
. Recall that
\alpha\leq\beta<0
and
\gamma>0
.

Proof.

Part (A) follows from Theorem
10.74
. Its proof uses only classical PDE tools and is completely standard. The remaining statements rely on probabilistic estimates established in Sec.
9
and deterministic construction presented in Sec.
10
. Parts (B) and (C) follow from Lemma
10.29
. Parts (D) and (E) are consequences of Theorem
10.76
.
∎

Remark 3.19
.

The method of the proof of the above theorem was discussed in Sec.
2
. Let us recall that it is based on the ansatz (
1.13
) for the effective force
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
and a differential equation of the form (
1.14
), called the flow equation, that governs the evolution of the effective force in the parameter
\mu
. The above-mentioned differential equation is closely related to the Polchinski flow equation in Euclidean QFT
[
45
]
(see also
[
48
]
) that governs the evolution of the effective potential in the cutoff scale. The Polchinski flow equation is very useful tool to investigate properties of models of perturbative QFT. There is a very simple and general proof of perturbative renormalizability of QFT models based on the Polchinski flow equation (see the review paper
[
40
]
; for the position space approach more similar to ours in this paper see
[
34
]
). In particular, the proof avoids completely the problem of overlapping divergences of Feynman integrals which is one of the major difficulties in the approaches based on the Feynman diagrammatical expansion (such as the BPHZ approach). For the state of the art applications of the Polchinski flow equation in perturbative QFT see
[
17
,
15
]
.

The Polchinski flow equation requires a certain formal power series ansatz for the effective potential. The above-mentioned series typically does not converge because of the so-called large field problem. As a result, the Polchinski flow equation was not used extensively in constructive quantum field theory. An exception is the Sine-Gordon model for which the perturbative series converges (for application of the flow equation for this model see
[
6
,
2
]
). Note that in the context of the initial value problem for the parabolic PDEs we avoid the large field problem by first constructing the solution in sufficiently small random time intervals.

Remark 3.20
.

The technique we use is related to the technique developed earlier by Kupiainen in
[
35
]
for the dynamical
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model and later applied to the generalized KPZ equation in
[
36
]
(a similar method was used earlier in
[
3
]
to study the long-time asymptotics of nonlinear parabolic PDEs). Both techniques are based on the Wilsonian renormalization group theory
[
49
]
. Let us briefly explain the Wilson approach to the renormalization problem adapted to the context of SPDEs. Consider an equation of the form

\varPhi_{\nu}=(G-G_{1})\ast F_{\nu}[\varPhi_{\nu}],

(3.14)

where
F_{\nu}[\varphi]
is some force and the parameter
\nu
plays the role of the UV cutoff. We note that
\varPhi_{\nu}=(G-G_{1})\ast f_{\nu,1}
is a solution of the above equation provided
f_{\nu,1}
is such that

f_{\nu,1}=F_{\nu}[(G-G_{1})\ast f_{\nu,1}].

(3.15)

Let
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
be the effective force satisfying Eq. (
1.15
) for every
\mu\in[0,1]
together with the boundary condition
F_{\nu,0}[\varphi]=F_{\nu}[\varphi]
. Then
f_{\nu,1}=F_{\nu,1}[0]
fulfills Eq. (
3.15
) and it holds

f_{\nu,1}=F_{\nu}[(G-G_{\mu})\ast f_{\nu,1}+(G_{\mu}-G_{1})\ast f_{\nu,1}]=F_{\nu,\mu}[(G_{\mu}-G_{1})\ast f_{\nu,1}]

(3.16)

for
\mu\in[0,1]
. Note that the characteristic temporal and spatial scales of the kernel
G_{\mu}
are of order
\mu
and
[\mu]=\mu^{1/\sigma}
, respectively. The functions
f_{\nu,1}
as well as
(G-G_{1})\ast f_{\nu,1}
and
(G-G_{\mu})\ast f_{\nu,1}
are essentially constant at temporal and spatial scales less than
\nu
and
[\nu]
. These functions converge as
\nu\searrow 0
only in the space of distributions. On the other hand, the function
(G_{\mu}-G_{1})\ast f_{\nu,1}
is essentially constant at temporal and spatial scales less than
\nu\vee\mu
and
[\nu\vee\mu]
and for every
\mu\in(0,1]
its limit
\nu\searrow 0
is a regular function. It is clear by Eq. (
3.16
) that in order to define the effective force
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
one has to control all potentially problematic products involving the rough part
(G-G_{\mu})\ast f_{\nu,1}
of the solution
\varPhi_{\nu}=(G-G_{1})\ast f_{\nu,1}
.

The basic idea of the Wilsonian renormalization group theory is to construct the effective force
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
for
\mu\in\{\delta^{n}\,|\,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, recursively using the fact that
F_{\nu,0}[\varphi]=F_{\nu}[\varphi]
and for
\eta\leq\mu
the effective forces
F_{\nu,\eta}[\varphi]
and
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
are related by Eq. (
1.15
). This is the approach taken in
[
35
,
36
]
in the context of singular SPDEs. The same strategy is used in most applications of the renormalization group in constructive QFT
[
19
,
20
]
. The discrete renormalization group method described above is very powerful. Unfortunately, constructions based on this method are typically highly technical. For this reason, we do not believe it would be feasible to give a general solution theory for a large class of sub-critical SPDEs using the technique of
[
35
,
36
]
.

Remark 3.21
.

Our approach to the renormalization problem is similar in spirit to the approach proposed recently in
[
37
]
in the context of quasi-linear SPDEs. The model introduced in that work consists of certain multi-linear functionals of the noise indexed by multi-indices. The above functionals can be represented by linear combinations of trees but this representation does not play any role in the proof. The stochastic estimates for the model are established inductively with the use of the spectral gap inequality. The counterterms are fixed by the symmetries and the BPHZ renormalization conditions.

Remark 3.22
.

Let us briefly describe the standard approaches that are used to give meaning to singular SPDEs. The case of equations such as the dynamical
\Phi^{4}_{2}
model for which Wick ordering is sufficient to define the non-linear term was resolved by Da Prato and Debussche
[
13
]
using an expansion around the solution of the linear equation. This type of expansion breaks down in the case of more singular equations like the dynamical
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model. The renormalization problem for such equations stayed open until the breakthrough by Hairer
[
26
]
, who introduced the theory of regularity structures allowing a local description of a distribution by a generalized Taylor expansion around each spacetime point. Another approach that can be used to tackle a large class of singular SPDEs was developed later by Gubinelli, Imkeller and Perkowski in
[
22
]
using the paracontrolled calculus. Let us also mention an approach based on the rough path theory proposed by Otto and Weber in
[
41
]
in the context of singular quasi-linear SPDEs.

Now we would like to discuss some applications of our result. In the situation when the physical system under consideration has some symmetries it is natural to assume that the noise and the force coefficients respect these symmetries. This requirement typically restricts the choice of the values of the renormalization parameters.

Definition 3.23
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a group and
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
be actions of
\mathcal{H}
on the space of spacetime functions
C(\mathbb{M})
such that
\mathbf{Q}\,\mathbf{M}_{g}\varphi=\mathbf{N}_{g}\mathbf{Q}\varphi
for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
. We say that the system has symmetry group
\mathcal{H}
if the noise
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{\nu}
and the microscopic force coefficients
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,m,a}_{\nu}
,
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}
, are such that the corresponding macroscopic force satisfies the condition

F_{\nu}[\mathbf{M}_{g}\varphi]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{(law)}}}{{=}}\mathbf{N}_{g}F_{\nu}[\varphi]

(3.17)

for every
\nu\in(0,1]
,
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
and
g\in\mathcal{H}
. We will only consider situation when the above condition implies automatically an analogous condition for the effective force
F_{\nu,\mu}[\varphi]
(we restrict attention to symmetries which are preserved by the cutoff propagator
G_{\mu}
).

Definition 3.24
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be the group of isometries of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
preserving the cubic lattice with spacing
2\pi
. The action of the group
\mathcal{G}
on the space of spacetime functions
C(\mathbb{M})
is defined by

(\mathbf{M}_{g}\varphi)(\mathring{x},\bar{x}):=\varphi(\mathring{x},g^{-1}\bar{x}),\qquad g\in\mathcal{G},\quad(\mathring{x},\bar{x})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}=\mathbb{M}.

(3.18)

The action of the group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on
C(\mathbb{M})
is defined by

(\mathbf{M}_{g}\varphi)(\mathring{x},\bar{x})=g\,\varphi(\mathring{x},\bar{x}),\qquad g\in\{-1,1\}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

(3.19)

We set
\mathbf{N}_{g}=\mathbf{M}_{g}
for all
g\in\mathcal{G}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Example 3.25
.

Let
\mathsf{d}=3
,
\sigma=2
and
\dim(\lambda)=1
. Consider any microscopic equation of the form (
1.1
) describing a system with the symmetry group
\mathcal{G}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. The above-mention equation describes a phase coexistence model studied in
[
29
,
46
]
(see also
[
18
,
16
,
50
]
). The only relevant force coefficients not violating the symmetries of the equation are

\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{1,3,0}_{\nu},\qquad\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{1,1,0}_{\nu},\qquad\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{2,1,0}_{\nu}.

(3.20)

The relevant force coefficients are fixed by the following renormalization conditions

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{1,3,0}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{1,3,0},\qquad\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{1,1,0}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{1,1,0},\qquad\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{2,1,0}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2,1,0}

(3.21)

and for all other
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=0.

(3.22)

Our analysis implies that the macroscopic behavior of this model is described by the following sub-critical singular SPDE

(\partial_{\mathring{x}}-\Delta_{\bar{x}})\varPhi_{0}(x)=\varXi_{0}(x)+\lambda\,f^{1,3,0}_{0}\varPhi_{0}(x)^{3}+\lambda f^{1,1,0}_{0}\varPhi_{0}(x)+\lambda^{2}f^{2,1,0}_{0}\varPhi_{0}(x),

(3.23)

where
\varXi_{0}
is the periodization of the white noise on
\mathbb{M}
,
f^{1,3,0}_{0}=\mathfrak{f}^{1,3,0}_{0}
and the mass counterterms
f^{1,1,0}_{0}
,
f^{2,1,0}_{0}
are not well-defined. This together with the a priori bounds proved in
[
38
]
allows us to recover the universality results established earlier in
[
46
]
.

Remark 3.26
.

The singular SPDE (
3.23
) is called the dynamical
\varPhi^{4}_{3}
model. Let us mention that the renormalization problem for this equation was solved for the first time in
[
26
]
and later in
[
7
,
35
]
. The universality class of the dynamical
\varPhi^{4}_{3}
model is expected to contain the three-dimensional Ising model with Glauber dynamics and Kac interactions near the critical temperature (this was conjectured in
[
21
]
; the two dimensional version of this result was established in
[
39
]
). The invariant measure of the above SPDE describes the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
Euclidean QFT (see for example
[
23
]
).

Remark 3.27
.

As mentioned above
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
is a bookkeeping parameter that can be set equal to one. In our approach the entire solution manifold of the dynamical
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model is parameterized by the initial data and the renormalization parameters
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{1,3,0},\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{1,1,0},\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2,1,0}\in\mathbb{R}
. However, this parametrization is not injective. It turns out that the whole solution manifold can be parameterized by the initial data and
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{1,3,0},\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2,1,0}\in\mathbb{R}
with fixed
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{1,1,0}=0
(it is natural to assume that
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{1,3,0}<0
to avoid finite time blow-up of the solution; in infinite volume one could in addition set
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{1,3,0}=-1
by a rescaling operation to get a one-parameter family of equations).

Example 3.28
.

Let
\mathsf{d}=4
,
\sigma\in(2,3)
and
\dim(\lambda)=2\sigma-4
. Consider any microscopic equation of the form (
1.1
) describing a system with the symmetry group
\mathcal{G}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. The only relevant force coefficients not violating the symmetries of the equation are

\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{1,3,0}_{\nu},\qquad\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,1,0}_{\nu},~~i\in\{1,\ldots,i_{\sharp}\},

(3.24)

where
i_{\sharp}:=\lfloor\sigma/\dim(\lambda)\rfloor
. Note that
i_{\sharp}\to\infty
as
\sigma\searrow 2
. The relevant force coefficients are fixed by the following renormalization conditions

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{1,3,0}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{1,3,0},\qquad\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,1,0}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{i,1,0},~~i\in\{1,\ldots,i_{\sharp}\},

(3.25)

and for all other
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=0.

(3.26)

The scaling limit
\varPhi_{0}:=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\nu}
formally solves the following sub-critical singular SPDE

\mathbf{Q}\varPhi_{0}(x)=\varXi_{0}(x)+\lambda\,f^{1,3,0}_{0}\varPhi_{0}(x)^{3}+\sum_{i=1}^{i_{\sharp}}\lambda^{i}f^{i,1,0}_{0}\varPhi_{0}(x).

(3.27)

Careful analysis shows that it holds
f^{1,3,0}_{0}=\mathfrak{f}^{1,3,0}_{0}
and the coefficients
f^{i,1,0}_{0}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,i_{\sharp}\}
, are generically ill-defined because the limit
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}f^{i,1,0}_{\nu}
does not exist (unless the equation is linear, that is
\mathfrak{f}^{1,3,0}_{0}=0
).
We obtain essentially the same result for all
\dim(\lambda)\in(\sigma-2,2\sigma-4]
.

Example 3.29
.

Let
\mathsf{d}=4
,
\sigma\in(2,8/3)
and
\dim(\lambda)=\sigma-2
. Consider again an arbitrary microscopic equation of the form (
1.1
) describing a system with the symmetry group
\mathcal{G}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. The only relevant force coefficients that are not fixed by the symmetries are

\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{1,1,\Delta}_{\nu}:=\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{1,1,a_{1}}_{\nu}=\ldots=\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{1,1,a_{\mathsf{d}}}_{\nu},\qquad\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,3,0}_{\nu},~~i\in\{1,2\}\qquad\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,1,0}_{\nu},~~i\in\{1,\ldots,i_{\sharp}\},

(3.28)

where the spatial multi-indices
a_{j}=(a_{j}^{1},\ldots,a_{j}^{\mathsf{d}})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathsf{d}}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{d}\}
, are defined by the conditions
a^{j}_{j}=2
and
a^{k}_{j}=0
for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{d}\}\setminus\{j\}
and
i_{\sharp}
is as in the previous example. We impose the following renormalization conditions

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{1,1,\Delta}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{1,1,\Delta},\qquad\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,3,0}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{i,3,0},~~i\in\{1,2\},

(3.29)

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,1,0}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{i,1,0},~~i\in\{1,\ldots,i_{\sharp}\},

(3.30)

and for all other
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=0.

(3.31)

The scaling limit
\varPhi_{0}:=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\nu}
formally solves the following sub-critical singular SPDE

\mathbf{Q}\varPhi_{0}(x)=\varXi_{0}(x)+\lambda f^{1,1,\Delta}_{0}\Delta_{\bar{x}}\varPhi_{0}(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\lambda^{i}f^{i,3,0}_{0}\varPhi_{0}(x)^{3}+\sum_{i=1}^{i_{\sharp}}\lambda^{i}f^{i,1,0}_{0}\varPhi_{0}(x).

(3.32)

Careful analysis shows that it holds
f^{1,1,\Delta}_{0}=\mathfrak{f}^{1,1,\Delta}_{0}
,
f^{1,3,0}_{0}=\mathfrak{f}^{1,3,0}_{0}
and the coefficients
f^{2,3,0}_{0}
and
f^{i,1,0}_{0}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,i_{\sharp}\}
, are generically ill-defined because the limits
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}f^{2,3,0}_{\nu}
and
\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}f^{i,1,0}_{\nu}
need not exist. If
\mathfrak{f}^{1,3,0}_{0}=0
, then
f^{2,3,0}_{0}=\mathfrak{f}^{2,3,0}_{0}
and
f^{1,1,0}_{0}=\mathfrak{f}^{1,1,0}_{0}
are well-defined. If in addition
\mathfrak{f}^{2,3,0}_{0}=0
, then the equation is linear and all coefficients are well-defined.
We obtain essentially the same result for all
\dim(\lambda)\in(0,\sigma-2]
.

Example 3.30
.

Let
\mathsf{d}=4
,
\sigma=2
and
\dim(\lambda)>0
. Consider again an arbitrary microscopic equation of the form (
1.1
) describing a system with the symmetry group
\mathcal{G}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. This time the only relevant force coefficients not violating the symmetries of the equation are

\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,1,0}_{\nu},~~i\in\{1,\ldots,i_{\sharp}\}.

(3.33)

We impose the following renormalization conditions

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,1,0}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{i,1,0},~~i\in\{1,\ldots,i_{\sharp}\}.

(3.34)

It is easy to see that

f^{i,1,0}_{0}=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}f^{i,1,0}_{\nu}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{i,1,0}

(3.35)

and the scaling limit
\varPhi_{0}:=\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\nu}
solves the following linear SPDE

\mathbf{Q}\varPhi_{0}(x)=\varXi_{0}(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{i_{\sharp}}\lambda^{i}f^{i,1,0}_{0}\,\varPhi_{0}(x).

(3.36)

Obviously the above triviality result is of little interest because of the restrictive assumption
\dim(\lambda)>0
.

Remark 3.31
.

If
\sigma\in[8/3,4)
, then depending on the choice of
\dim(\lambda)>0
there are other relevant force coefficients not violating the symmetries of the equation, for example
f^{1,5,0}_{\nu}
. If
\sigma\geq\mathsf{d}=4
, then the number of possible relevant force coefficients is not bounded and no universality result is expected.

Remark 3.32
.

If the spatial dimension
\mathsf{d}
equals
4
, as assumed above, then our technique allows to renormalize the singular SPDE (
3.27
) in the whole range of subcriticality
\sigma\in(2,4]
(for
\sigma>4
the above equation is not singular). If
\mathsf{d}=3
, then the SPDE (
3.27
) is singular and sub-critical for
\sigma\in(3/2,3]
. In the case
\mathsf{d}=3
our method covers only the range
\sigma\in(5/3,3]
. In general, for
\sigma\notin 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
because of the long-range character of the fractional heat kernel our method needs the following assumption
\lceil\sigma\rceil-1>\sigma-2\dim(\lambda)
, see Assumption
3.13
.

Remark 3.33
.

For
\mathsf{d}=3
and
\sigma=2
the singular SPDE (
3.27
) coincides with the dynamical
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model. For
\mathsf{d}=3
and
\sigma\in(21/11,2)
the renormalization problem for the SPDE (
3.27
) is very similar to the renormalization problem for the dynamical
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model and was solved in
[
23
]
using paracontrolled calculus. Different types of non-local singular SPDEs were previously studied in
[
22
,
32
,
11
,
33
]
. However, the non-local SPDEs considered in these references are not close to criticality.

Remark 3.34
.

There exists a general blackbox machinery built upon the theory of regularity structures that can be used to systematically renormalize virtually all sub-critical singular SPDEs with local differential operators
[
8
,
5
,
4
]
. It is applicable also in the case
\dim(\varPhi)<0
which is not covered by our technique. However, its generalization for non-local singular SPDEs treated in the present work is non-trivial. Let us also mention that there are a number of results about specific families of singular SPDEs covering whole sub-critical region
[
9
,
10
,
42
,
37
]
.

Remark 3.35
.

The singular SPDE (
3.27
) belongs to the class of the stochastic quantization equations
[
43
,
12
]
. The invariant measure for the SPDE (
3.27
) describes a model of QFT with a modified propagator (this was established in
[
23
]
for
\mathsf{d}=3
and
\sigma\in(21/11,2]
). Models of this type (especially for
\mathsf{d}=3
) attracted attention in the area of constructive QFT because they can be used to implement rigorously the Wilson-Fischer expansion used to study critical phenomena (see for example
[
1
]
and references cited therein). In the case
\mathsf{d}=4
the invariant measure for the SPDE (
3.27
) is not supposed to be reflection positive but should satisfy all other Osterwalder-Schrader axioms. Let us mention that the SPDEs of the form (
3.27
) are expected to describe the large scale behavior of certain long-range statistical models. For example, the SPDEs of the above form should govern the random fluctuations of a suitably rescaled coarse-grained spin field of the Ising-Kac model with power-law-decaying interactions of the form
|\bar{x}|^{-\mathsf{d}-\sigma}
, where
|\bar{x}|
is the distance between the spins. However, we are not aware of any rigorous results in this direction.

Definition 3.36
.

The actions
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
of the group
\mathbb{R}
on
C(\mathbb{M})
are defined by

(\mathbf{M}_{g}\varphi)(\mathring{x},\bar{x})=\varphi(\mathring{x},\bar{x})+g,\qquad(\mathbf{N}_{g}\varphi)(\mathring{x},\bar{x})=\varphi(\mathring{x},\bar{x}),\qquad g\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.37)

Example 3.37
.

Let
\mathsf{d}=1
,
\sigma=2
and
\dim(\lambda)=1/2
. Note that, strictly speaking, this example is outside the scope of the technique developed in this paper since
\dim(\varPhi)=(\mathsf{d}-\sigma)/2<0
. Consider an arbitrary microscopic equation of the form

(\partial_{\mathring{x}}-\Delta_{\bar{x}})\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Phi}}_{\nu}(x)=\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{F}}}_{\nu}[\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Phi}}_{\nu}](x),\qquad x=(\mathring{x},\bar{x})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}

(3.38)

describing a system with the symmetry group
\mathcal{G}\times\mathbb{R}
with broken
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry. The equations of the above type belong to the class of continuous interface growth models that were studied in
[
27
,
30
]
(see also
[
31
]
as well as
[
24
]
and references cited therein). Since the system preserves the
\mathbb{R}
symmetry the force
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{F}}}_{\nu}[\varphi]
can depend on
\varphi
only thorough its spatial derivative
\partial_{\bar{x}}\varphi
. Consequently, there exists another force
\dot{\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{F}}}}_{\nu}[\varphi]
of the form (
1.2
) such that it holds

\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{F}}}_{\nu}[\varphi]:=\dot{\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{F}}}}_{\nu}[\partial_{\bar{x}}\varphi]

(3.39)

for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
. Consider the microscopic equation

(\partial_{\mathring{x}}-\Delta_{\bar{x}})\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Psi}}_{\nu}(x)=\partial_{\bar{x}}\dot{\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{F}}}}_{\nu}[\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Psi}}_{\nu}](x),\qquad x=(\mathring{x},\bar{x})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R},

(3.40)

which is related to Eq. (
3.38
) via the identification
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Psi}}_{\nu}=\partial_{\bar{x}}\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Phi}}_{\nu}
. As described briefly in Remark
3.39
our technique can be adapted to yield the existence and universality of the macroscopic scaling limit for the above equation. The only relevant coefficients of the force
\dot{\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{F}}}}_{\nu}[\varphi]
not violating the symmetries of the equation are

\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{1,2,0}_{\nu},\qquad\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{1,0,0}_{\nu},\qquad\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{2,0,0}_{\nu},\qquad\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{3,0,0}_{\nu}.

(3.41)

The relevant force coefficients are fixed by the following renormalization conditions

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{1,2,0}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{1,2,0},\qquad\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,0,0}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{i,0,0},\quad i\in\{1,2,3\}

(3.42)

and for all other
(i,m,a)\in\bar{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}

\lim_{\nu\searrow 0}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}f^{i,m,a}_{\nu,1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.33336pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=0.

(3.43)

We note that the solution of Eq. (
3.40
) does not depend on the choice of the coefficients
\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\mathrm{f}}}^{i,0,0}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. The macroscopic scaling limit is described by the following singular SPDE

(\partial_{\mathring{x}}-\Delta_{\bar{x}})\varPsi_{0}(x)=\partial_{\bar{x}}\big(\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{0}(x)+\lambda f^{1,2,0}_{0}\varPsi_{0}(x)^{2}+\lambda f^{1,0,0}_{0}+\lambda^{2}f^{2,0,0}_{0}+\lambda^{3}f^{3,0,0}_{0}\big),

(3.44)

where
f^{1,2,0}_{0}=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{1,2,0}
and the counterterms
f^{i,0,0}_{0}
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, need not be well-defined. This allows us to essentially reproduce the universality results of
[
27
,
30
]
in the weakly asymmetric regime.

Remark 3.38
.

The singular SPDE (
3.44
) is called the stochastic Burgers equation and is obtained by taking the spatial derivative of both sides of the KPZ equation

(\partial_{\mathring{x}}-\Delta_{\bar{x}})\varPhi_{0}(x)=\mathrm{\underaccent{\bar}{\Xi}}_{0}(x)+\lambda f^{1,2,0}_{0}(\partial_{\bar{x}}\varPhi_{0}(x))^{2}+\lambda f^{1,0,0}_{0}+\lambda^{2}f^{2,0,0}_{0}+\lambda^{3}f^{3,0,0}_{0}.

(3.45)

One can define the notion of solution of the above equation using the Cole-Hopf transform and the classical Itô calculus (for the Cole-Hopf solution it holds
f^{2,0,0}_{0}=f^{3,0,0}_{0}=0
and
f^{1,0,0}_{0}
is a certain infinite Itô correction). The robust approximation theory for KPZ equation was first given in
[
25
]
. Let us note that there are many microscopic models for which the convergence to the KPZ equation has been established rigorously (see the references cited in
[
30
]
).

Remark 3.39
.

In order to prove that the large-scale behavior of solutions of the SPDE (
3.40
) is described by to the stochastic Burgers equation we study the following equation

\varPsi_{\nu}=\partial_{\bar{x}}G\ast(1_{(0,\infty)}F_{\nu}[\varPsi_{\nu}]+\delta_{0}\otimes\phi_{\nu}),

(3.46)

where
G
is the heat kernel in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
. We apply the technique develop in the paper with the following (non-standard) choice of the dimensions
\dim(\varPsi)=-1/2
,
\dim(\varXi)=-3/2
,
\dim(\lambda)=1/2
(recall that
\mathsf{d}=1
,
\sigma=2
). Note that
\partial_{\bar{x}}G
is a regularizing kernel of order
\dim(\varXi)-\dim(\varPsi)=1
in the scale of the parabolic Besov spaces.

4
Regularizing kernels

In this section we define kernels that will be used to control the regularity of the effective force coefficients and their cumulants. We also introduce the scale decomposition of the fractional heat kernel. We prove several properties of the regularizing kernels and the scale decomposition of the fractional heat kernel, which are frequently used in the paper.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. The space of compactly supported test functions is denoted by
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. The space of bounded smooth functions in denoted by
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. The space of Schwartz functions is denoted by
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. The space of distributions and the subspace of tempered distributions are denoted by
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, respectively. The Fourier transform in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is denoted by
\mathbf{F}
. In particular, for a Schwartz function
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
we define

\mathbf{F}\varphi(p)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,p\cdot x)\,\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad\varphi(x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\exp(\mathrm{i}\,p\cdot x)\,\mathbf{F}\varphi(p)\,\mathrm{d}p.

(4.1)

Observe that

\mathbf{F}(\psi\ast\varphi)(p)=\mathbf{F}(\psi)(p)\,\mathbf{F}(\varphi)(p)

(4.2)

for
\psi,\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, where
\ast
denotes the convolution. We use the following notation
\delta_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
for the Dirac delta. We also set
\mathbb{R}^{0}=\{0\}
.

Definition 4.1
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
let
\mathcal{K}^{n}\subset\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{n})
be the space of signed measures
K
on
\mathbb{M}^{n}
with finite total variation
|K|
. We set
\|K\|_{\mathcal{K}^{n}}=\int_{\mathbb{M}^{n}}|K(\mathrm{d}x_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}x_{n})|
. If
n=1
, then we write
\mathcal{K}^{1}=\mathcal{K}\subset\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
. Given
K\in\mathcal{K}
we set
K^{\otimes 0}:=\delta_{\mathbb{M}}
and
K^{\otimes n}:=K\otimes\ldots\otimes K\in\mathcal{K}^{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.

Definition 4.2
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
\mu>0
and
K\in C(\mathbb{M}^{n})
we define

\mathbf{S}_{\mu}K(\mathring{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathring{x}_{n},\bar{x}_{n}):=[\mu]^{-\mathsf{D}n}\,K(\mathring{x}_{1}/\mu,\bar{x}_{1}/[\mu],\ldots,\mathring{x}_{n}/\mu,\bar{x}_{n}/[\mu]),

(4.3)

where
[\mu]:=\mu^{1/\sigma}
. The map
\mathbf{S}_{\mu}
extends to
K\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{n})
in natural way.

Remark 4.3
.

For any
\mu>0
the map
\mathbf{S}_{\mu}\,:\mathcal{K}^{n}\to\mathcal{K}^{n}
is an isometry.

Definition 4.4
.

For
K\in\mathcal{K}
we set
K^{\ast 0}:=\delta_{\mathbb{M}}
and
K^{\ast({\mathtt{g}}+1)}:=K\ast K^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}
for
{\mathtt{g}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. For an operator
\mathbf{P}
we set
\mathbf{P}^{0}:=\mathrm{id}
and
\mathbf{P}^{{\mathtt{g}}+1}:=\mathbf{P}\,\mathbf{P}^{{\mathtt{g}}}
for
{\mathtt{g}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Definition 4.5
.

We define
\varepsilon_{\sigma}:=\sigma+1-\lceil\sigma\rceil\in(0,1\wedge\sigma]
.

Remark 4.6
.

In what follows, we assume that
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\sigma})
. Later we shall introduce other possibly smaller strictly positive upper bounds for
\varepsilon
.

Definition 4.7
.

For
\mu\in(0,\infty)
the kernels
K_{\mu},J_{\mu}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{M})\subset\mathcal{K}
are defined by

\mathbf{F}K_{\mu}(p)=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{i}\mu\mathring{p}}\frac{1}{1+[\mu]^{2}|\bar{p}|^{2}},\qquad\mathbf{F}J_{\mu}(p)=\frac{1}{1+[\mu]^{\sigma-\varepsilon}|\bar{p}|^{\sigma-\varepsilon}}.

(4.4)

For
\mu\in(0,\infty)
the kernels
\mathring{K}_{\mu}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
,
\bar{K}_{\mu}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
are defined by

\mathbf{F}\mathring{K}_{\mu}(\mathring{p}):=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{i}\mu\mathring{p}},\qquad\mathbf{F}\bar{K}_{\mu}(\bar{p}):=\frac{1}{1+[\mu]^{2}|\bar{p}|^{2}}.

(4.5)

We omit the index
\mu
if
\mu=1
. We also set
K_{0}=\delta_{\mathbb{M}}
,
J_{0}=\delta_{\mathbb{M}}
,
\mathring{K}_{0}=\delta_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\bar{K}_{0}=\delta_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}}
.

Definition 4.8
.

For
\mu\in[0,\infty)
we define the following (pseudo-)differential operators

\mathbf{P}_{\mu}\equiv\mathbf{P}_{\mu}(\partial_{x})=(1+\mu\partial_{\mathring{x}})(1-[\mu]^{2}\Delta_{\bar{x}}),\quad\mathbf{R}_{\mu}\equiv\mathbf{R}_{\mu}(\partial_{x})=1+[\mu]^{\sigma-\varepsilon}(-\Delta_{\bar{x}})^{(\sigma-\varepsilon)/2},

(4.6)

\mathring{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}\equiv\mathring{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}(\partial_{x})=1+\mu\partial_{\mathring{x}},\qquad\bar{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}\equiv\bar{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}(\partial_{x})=1-[\mu]^{2}\Delta_{\bar{x}}.

(4.7)

We omit the index
\mu
if
\mu=1
.

Remark 4.9
.

For
\mu\in(0,\infty)
we have
K_{\mu}=\mathbf{S}_{\mu}K
,
J_{\mu}=\mathbf{S}_{\mu}J
. For
{\mathtt{g}}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
\mu\in[0,\infty)
the kernels
K_{\mu}^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}
,
\mathring{K}_{\mu}^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}
,
\bar{K}_{\mu}^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}
are positive measures with total mass equal to
1
. Moreover, we have
K_{\mu}^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}=\mathring{K}_{\mu}^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}\otimes\bar{K}_{\mu}^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}
,
\mathbf{P}^{\mathtt{g}}_{\mu}=\mathring{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathtt{g}}_{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathtt{g}}_{\mu}
and

\mathbf{P}^{\mathtt{g}}_{\mu}K^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mu}=\delta_{\mathbb{M}},\quad\mathring{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathtt{g}}_{\mu}\mathring{K}^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mu}=\delta_{\mathbb{R}},\quad\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathtt{g}}_{\mu}\bar{K}^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mu}=\delta_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}},\quad\mathbf{R}^{\mathtt{g}}_{\mu}J^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mu}=\delta_{\mathbb{M}}.

(4.8)

The fact that
K
and
J
are the inverses of (pseudo-)differential operators plays an important role in our construction.

Lemma 4.10
.

For any
\mu,\eta>0
,

\begin{gathered}K_{\mu}=\mathbf{P}_{\eta}K_{\mu}\ast K_{\eta}\qquad\|\mathbf{P}_{\eta}K_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=1\vee(2\eta/\mu-1)(2[\eta/\mu]^{2}-1).\end{gathered}

(4.9)

In particular, if
\mu\geq\eta
, then
\|\mathbf{P}_{\eta}K_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=1
.

Proof.

By the exact scaling relations it is enough to consider the case
\mu=1
. The statement of the lemma follows from the identity

\mathbf{P}_{\eta}K=(\eta\delta_{\mathbb{M}}+(1-\eta)\mathring{K}\otimes\delta_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}})\ast([\eta]^{2}\delta_{\mathbb{M}}+(1-[\eta]^{2})\delta_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes\bar{K})

(4.10)

and
\|\delta_{\mathbb{M}}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=1
,
\|\mathring{K}\otimes\delta_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=1
,
\|\delta_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes\bar{K}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=1
.
∎

Definition 4.11
.

For
H\in L^{1}(\mathbb{M})
we define
\mathbf{T}H\in L^{1}(\mathbb{H})
by the formula

\mathbf{T}H(\mathring{x},\bar{x}):=\sum_{\bar{y}\in(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{\mathsf{d}}}V(\mathring{x},\bar{x}+\bar{y}).

(4.11)

For
H\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
we define
\mathbf{T}H\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T})
by an analogous formula.

Remark 4.12
.

For future reference, we note that
H\ast f=\mathbf{T}H\star f
for
f\in C(\mathbb{H})
and
H\in L^{1}(\mathbb{M})
, where
\ast
and
\star
are the convolutions in
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{H}
, respectively.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\mathtt{f}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
a\in\mathfrak{M}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
.

(A)

If
|a|\leq\mathtt{f}
, then
\|\partial^{a}K^{\ast\mathtt{f}}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\mu]^{-[a]}
uniformly in
\mu>0
.

(B)

If
a\in\bar{\mathfrak{M}}_{\sigma}
, then
\|\partial^{a}J_{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\nu]^{-[a]}
uniformly in
\nu>0
.

(C)

If
2\mathtt{f}>\sigma
, then
\|\mathbf{R}^{\phantom{\mathtt{f}}}_{\nu}\bar{K}_{\mu}^{\ast\mathtt{f}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})}\lesssim[\nu\vee\mu]^{\sigma-\varepsilon}\,[\mu]^{\varepsilon-\sigma}
uniformly in
\nu,\mu>0
.

(D)

If
\mathsf{d}-\mathtt{f}<\mathsf{d}/p
, then
\|\mathbf{T}\bar{K}^{\ast\mathtt{f}}_{\mu}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}\lesssim[\mu]^{-\mathsf{d}(p-1)/p}
uniformly in
\mu\in(0,1]
.

Remark 4.14
.

Recall that
K_{\mu}=\mathring{K}_{\mu}\otimes\bar{K}_{\mu}
and
\mathring{K}_{\mu}(\mathring{x})=\mu^{-1}\,1_{[0,\infty)}(\mathring{x})\exp(-\mathring{x}/\mu)
. As a result, Part (C) of the above lemma implies that if
2\mathtt{f}>\sigma
, then

\|\mathbf{R}^{\phantom{\mathtt{f}}}_{\nu}K_{\mu}^{\ast\mathtt{f}}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\nu\vee\mu]^{\sigma-\varepsilon}\,[\mu]^{\varepsilon-\sigma}

uniformly in
\nu,\mu>0
and Part (D) implies that if
\mathsf{d}-\mathtt{f}<\mathsf{d}/p
, then

\|\mathbf{T}K^{\ast\mathtt{f}}_{\mu}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{H})}\lesssim[\mu]^{-\mathsf{D}(p-1)/p}

uniformly in
\mu\in(0,1]
. Recall also that the kernels
\bar{K}^{\ast\mathtt{f}}_{\mu}
and
K^{\ast\mathtt{f}}_{\mu}
are positive. Hence,
\|\mathbf{T}|\bar{K}^{\ast\mathtt{f}}_{\mu}|\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}=\|\mathbf{T}\bar{K}^{\ast\mathtt{f}}_{\mu}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}
and
\|\mathbf{T}|K^{\ast\mathtt{f}}_{\mu}|\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{H})}=\|\mathbf{T}K^{\ast\mathtt{f}}_{\mu}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{H})}
.

Remark 4.15
.

Let
f\in C(\mathbb{M})
,
{\mathtt{g}}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
a\in\mathfrak{M}
be such that
|a|\leq{\mathtt{g}}
. We will often use the fact that
K^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mu}\ast f\in C^{\mathtt{g}}(\mathbb{M})
and
\|\partial^{a}K^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mu}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{M})}\lesssim[\mu]^{-[a]}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{M})}
uniformly over
\mu\in(0,\infty)
and
f\in C(\mathbb{M})
.

Proof.

We use the relations
K_{\mu}=\mathbf{S}_{\mu}K
,
J_{\nu}=\mathbf{S}_{\nu}J
. Part (A) follows from the fact that
\partial^{a}K\in\mathcal{K}
for
|a|\leq 1
. To prove Part (B) note that
a=(\mathring{a},\bar{a})\in\bar{\mathfrak{M}}_{\sigma}
iff
\mathring{a}=0
and
|\bar{a}|<\sigma
. Hence, by Remark
4.6

|\bar{a}|<\sigma-\varepsilon
and by Lemma
4.24
(C)
\partial^{a}J\in\mathcal{K}
. Part (C) is a consequence of Lemma
4.24
(D).

Let us turn to the proof of Part (D). First, note that the special case
\mathtt{f}=1
imply the general case by the identity
\mathbf{T}\bar{K}^{\ast(\mathtt{f}+1)}_{\mu}=\mathbf{T}\bar{K}^{\ast\mathtt{f}}_{\mu}\star\mathbf{T}\bar{K}_{\mu}
and the Young inequality for convolution, where
\star
is the convolution in
\mathbb{T}
. Identifying
\mathbb{T}
with
[-\pi,\pi)^{\mathsf{d}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
we get

\|\mathbf{T}\bar{K}_{\mu}-\bar{K}_{\mu}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}\lesssim[\mu]^{q}

(4.12)

for any
p\in[1,\infty]
and
q>0
since
\bar{K}(\bar{x})\lesssim\exp(-|\bar{x}|)
uniformly in
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
for
|\bar{x}|>1
. Because
|\bar{x}|^{\mathsf{d}-1}\bar{K}(\bar{x})
is bounded we obtain
\bar{K}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
for any
p\in[1,\mathsf{d}/(\mathsf{d}-1))
and

\|\bar{K}_{\mu}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\|\bar{K}_{\mu}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})}=[\mu]^{-\mathsf{d}(p-1)/p}\|\bar{K}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})}\lesssim[\mu]^{-\mathsf{d}(p-1)/p}.

(4.13)

The statement follows from the trivial identity
\mathbf{T}K_{\mu}=K_{\mu}+(\mathbf{T}K_{\mu}-K_{\mu})
and the bounds proved above.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

For
\varepsilon\in[0,2\wedge\sigma]
we have
\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu}\partial_{\mu}K_{\mu}\ast K_{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\mu]^{\varepsilon-\sigma}[\mu\vee\eta]^{-\varepsilon}
uniformly in
\mu\in(0,1]
and
\eta\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}:=\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{P}_{\mu}
and
\dot{\mathbf{P}}=\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{1}
. We note that
\mathbf{P}_{\mu}\partial_{\mu}K_{\mu}\ast K_{\eta}=-\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}K_{\mu}\ast K_{\eta}
. The Fourier transform of
\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}K
coincides with

\frac{i\mathring{p}}{1+i\mathring{p}}\frac{1+[\mu]^{2}|\bar{p}|^{2}}{1+|\bar{p}|^{2}}+\frac{2}{\sigma}\frac{1+\mu i\mathring{p}}{1+i\mathring{p}}\frac{[\mu]^{2-\sigma}|\bar{p}|^{2}}{1+|\bar{p}|^{2}}.

(4.14)

As a result,
\|\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}K\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\mu]^{2}\vee[\mu]^{2-\sigma}\vee 1
uniformly for
\mu\in(0,\infty)
. Since

\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu}\partial_{\mu}K_{\mu}\ast K_{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=\|\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}K_{\mu}\ast K_{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=\|K_{\mu}\ast\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}K_{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\leq\|\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}K_{\mu\vee\eta}\|_{\mathcal{K}}

(4.15)

the statement follows from the equality
\|\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}K_{\kappa}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=\kappa^{-1}\|\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu/\kappa}K\|_{\mathcal{K}}
.
∎

Remark 4.17
.

By the above lemma
\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu}\partial_{\mu}K_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\mu]^{-\sigma}
,
\|\partial_{\mu}K_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\mu]^{-\sigma}
and
\|\partial_{\mu}K_{\mu}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{M})}\lesssim[\mu]^{-\sigma}\,\|K_{\mu}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{M})}
uniformly in
\mu\in(0,1]
and
f\in C(\mathbb{M})
.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
N\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
. For
\mu\in(0,1]
set
N_{\mu}:=\mathbf{S}_{\mu}N
, where the rescaling operator
\mathbf{S}_{\mu}
was introduced in Def.
4.2
. For any
a\in\mathfrak{M}
and
r\in\{0,1\}
:

(A)

\|\partial^{a}\partial_{\mu}^{r}N_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\mu]^{-[a]-r\sigma}
uniformly in
\mu\in(0,1]
,

(B)

\|\partial_{\mu}N_{\mu}\ast K_{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\mu]^{\varepsilon-\sigma}\,[\eta]^{-\varepsilon}
uniformly in
\mu,\eta\in(0,1]
for all
\varepsilon\in[0,1\wedge\sigma]
.

Proof.

Part (A) follows from Def.
4.2
of the map
\mathbf{S}_{\mu}
. Part (B) for
\mu>\eta
follows from Part (A) and
\|K_{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=1
. To prove part (B) for
\mu\leq\eta
it is enough to use the uniform bound
\|\partial^{a}K_{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\eta]^{-[a]}
for
a\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
|a|=1
and the identity

\partial_{\mu}N_{\mu}=-\partial_{\mathring{x}}\mathbf{S}_{\mu}\mathring{N}-\sigma^{-1}[\mu]^{1-\sigma}\textstyle\sum_{p=1}^{\mathsf{d}}\partial_{x^{p}}\mathbf{S}_{\mu}\bar{N}^{p},

(4.16)

where
\mathring{N}(\mathring{x},\bar{x}):=\mathring{x}N(\mathring{x},\bar{x})
and
\bar{N}^{p}(\mathring{x},\bar{x}):=\bar{x}^{p}N(\mathring{x},\bar{x})
for
p\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{d}\}
.
∎

Definition 4.19
.

Fix a function
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that such that
\mathrm{supp}\,\chi\subset(1,\infty)
and
\chi=1
on some neighborhood of
[2,\infty)
. For
\mu\in(0,\infty)
and
a\in\mathfrak{M}
we define

G_{\mu}(\mathring{x},\bar{x}):=\chi(\mathring{x}/\mu)\,G(\mathring{x},\bar{x}),\qquad\dot{G}_{\mu}:=\partial_{\mu}G_{\mu},\qquad\dot{G}_{\mu}^{a}:=\mathcal{X}^{a}\dot{G}_{\mu},

(4.17)

where
\mathcal{X}^{a}(x):=x^{a}
is a polynomial in
x\in\mathbb{M}
and the fractional heat kernel
G\in\mathcal{K}
is defined by

\mathbf{F}G(\mathring{x},{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})(\bar{p})=1_{[0,\infty)}(\mathring{x})\,\exp(-\mathring{x}\,|\bar{p}|^{\sigma}),\qquad\mathring{x}\in\mathbb{R}.

(4.18)

We also set
G_{0}:=G
.

Remark 4.20
.

The kernel
G
is supported in
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
and is smooth outside
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
(outside the origin if
\sigma\in 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
). We have

\dot{G}^{a}_{\mu}(\mathring{x},\bar{x})=-\mathring{x}/\mu^{2}\,\dot{\chi}(\mathring{x}/\mu)\,(\mathcal{X}^{a}G)(\mathring{x},\bar{x}),\qquad\dot{\chi}(t):=\partial_{t}\chi(t).

(4.19)

Observe that for
\mu>0
it holds
\mathrm{supp}\,G_{\mu}\subset(\mu,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
,
\mathrm{supp}\,\dot{G}_{\mu}\subset(\mu,2\mu)\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
and
G_{\mu}=\mu\,\mathbf{S}_{\mu}(G_{1})
,
\dot{G}_{\mu}=\mathbf{S}_{\mu}(\dot{G}_{1})
.

Remark 4.21
.

For any
b=(\mathring{b},\bar{b})\in\mathfrak{M}
it holds

\mathbf{F}(\partial^{b}G)(1,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})(\bar{p})=(-1)^{\mathring{b}}\,\mathrm{i}^{|\bar{b}|}\,|\bar{p}|^{\sigma\mathring{b}}\,\bar{p}^{\bar{b}}\exp(-|\bar{p}|^{\sigma}),

(4.20)

By Lemma
4.24
(E)
(\partial^{b}G)(1,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
and
(1+|\bar{x}|^{\sigma+\mathsf{d}+|\bar{b}|})\,|(\partial^{b}G)(1,\bar{x})|\lesssim 1
uniformly in
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
. If
\sigma\in 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
, then
\mathbf{F}(\partial^{b}G)(1,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
and consequently
(\partial^{b}G)(1,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
. By the scaling property of the fractional heat kernel we obtain

\partial^{b}G(x)=[\mathring{x}]^{-\mathsf{d}-[b]}(\partial^{b}G)(1,\bar{x}/[\mathring{x}]),\qquad x=(\mathring{x},\bar{x})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}.

(4.21)

This shows that the kernel
G
is smooth outside
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
(outside the origin if
\sigma\in 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
) and the kernels
\partial^{a}(G-G_{\mu})
and
\partial^{b}\dot{G}^{c}_{\mu}
belong to
\mathcal{K}
for any
\mu\in(0,\infty)
,
a\in\bar{\mathfrak{M}}_{\sigma}
,
b\in\mathfrak{M}
,
c\in\mathfrak{M}_{\sigma}
. Moreover, for any
a=(\mathring{a},\bar{a}),b=(\mathring{b},\bar{b})\in\mathfrak{M}
the following bound

|\partial^{b}(\mathcal{X}^{a}G)(x)|\lesssim|\mathring{x}|^{1+\mathring{a}-\mathring{b}}\,|x|_{\mathbb{M}}^{|\bar{a}|-|\bar{b}|-\sigma-\mathsf{d}}

(4.22)

holds uniformly in
x\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
, where
|x|_{\mathbb{M}}=[\mathring{x}]\vee|\bar{x}|
and
[\mathring{x}]=|\mathring{x}|^{1/\sigma}
.

Remark 4.22
.

Recall that
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}_{\sigma}
consists of spatial multi-indices
a
such that
|a|<\sigma
and
\mathfrak{M}_{\sigma}
consists of space-time multi-indices
a
such that
|a|\leq\sigma_{\diamond}
, where
\sigma_{\diamond}=\sigma
if
\sigma\in 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
\sigma_{\diamond}=\lceil\sigma\rceil-1
otherwise.

Lemma 4.23
.

For any
{\mathtt{g}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a\in\mathfrak{M}_{\sigma}
it holds

\|\mathbf{R}^{\phantom{{\mathtt{g}}}}_{\nu}\mathbf{P}_{\mu}^{\mathtt{g}}\dot{G}^{a}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\nu\vee\mu]^{\sigma-\varepsilon}\,[\mu]^{\varepsilon-\sigma+[a]},

(4.23)

uniformly in
\nu\in[0,\infty)
and
\mu\in(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

We first show that

\|\partial^{b}\dot{G}^{a}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\mu]^{[a]-[b]}

(4.24)

for all
a\in\mathfrak{M}_{\sigma}
and all
b\in\mathfrak{M}
. Because
\partial^{b}\dot{G}_{\mu}^{a}=[\mu]^{[a]-[b]}\,\mathbf{S}_{\mu}(\partial^{b}\dot{G}_{1}^{a})
, where the rescaling operator
\mathbf{S}_{\nu}
was introduced in Def.
4.2
, it is enough to prove that
\partial^{b}(\mathcal{X}^{a}\dot{G}_{1})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{M})
for all
a\in\mathfrak{M}_{\sigma}
and all
b\in\mathfrak{M}
, which follows from Remark
4.21
. Using the above bound as well as the equalities
[b]=\sigma\mathring{b}+|\bar{b}|
for any
b=(\mathring{b},\bar{b})\in\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\mu}=(1+[\mu]^{\sigma}\partial_{\mathring{x}})(1-[\mu]^{2}\Delta_{\bar{x}})
we obtain the statement of the lemma with
\nu=0
. The general case follows from Lemma
4.13
(C) and the estimate

\|\mathbf{R}^{\phantom{{\mathtt{g}}}}_{\nu}\mathbf{P}_{\mu}^{\mathtt{g}}\dot{G}^{a}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\leq\|\mathbf{R}^{\phantom{{\mathtt{g}}}}_{\nu}K_{\mu}^{\ast\mathtt{f}}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\,\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu}^{{\mathtt{g}}+\mathtt{f}}\dot{G}^{a}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}

(4.25)

applied with
\mathtt{f}=\lfloor\sigma/2\rfloor+1
.
∎

We conclude this section with a technical lemma that was used several times above.

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
,
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathsf{d}}
,
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
H\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
.

(A)

If
\mathsf{d}+|a|+\vartheta>0
and
\mathbf{F}H(\bar{p})=p^{a}|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta}
, then
|\bar{x}|^{\mathsf{d}+|a|+\vartheta}H(\bar{x})
is smooth and bounded in the domain
\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}\setminus\{0\}
.

(B)

If
(-\Delta_{\bar{p}})^{n}\mathbf{F}H(\bar{p})
is integrable, then
|\bar{x}|^{2n}H(\bar{x})
is bounded and continuous.

(C)

If
|a|<\vartheta
and
\mathbf{F}H(\bar{p})=p^{a}/(1+|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta})
, then
H\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
.

(D)

If
\vartheta\in[0,2)
and
\mathbf{F}H(\bar{p})=|\bar{p}|^{2m+\vartheta}/(1+|\bar{p}|^{2})^{m+1}
, then
H\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
.

(E)

If
\vartheta\in(0,\infty)
and
\mathbf{F}H(\bar{p})=\bar{p}^{a}\,|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta m}\,\exp(-|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta})
, then
H\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
and
|\bar{x}|^{\mathsf{d}+|a|+\vartheta}H(\bar{x})
is bounded.

Proof.

Parts (A) and (B) are well-known facts.

To prove Part (C) first note that if
2\vartheta>\mathsf{d}+2|a|
, then
\mathbf{F}H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
and
H
is locally integrable. Otherwise let
k
be the largest integer such that
\vartheta k<\mathsf{d}+|a|
. Since
2\vartheta\leq\mathsf{d}+2|a|
and
\vartheta>|a|
it holds
\vartheta<\mathsf{d}+|a|
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. Observe that

\mathbf{F}H(\bar{p})=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{l-1}\bar{p}^{a}}{|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta l}}+\frac{(-1)^{k}\bar{p}^{a}}{|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta k}(1+|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta})}.

(4.26)

Using Part (A) and noting that the last term in the above decomposition is in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
we infer that
H
is locally integrable. If
\vartheta
is rational, then let
k
be the smallest positive integer such that
\vartheta k\in 2\mathbb{N}_{+}
and let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
be such that
\vartheta(k-1)+|a|<2n-\mathsf{d}
. Otherwise let
n
be the smallest positive integer such that
|a|<2n-\mathsf{d}
and let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
be such that
\vartheta(k-1)+|a|<2n-\mathsf{d}<\vartheta k+|a|
. Using the identity

\mathbf{F}H(\bar{p})=\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}~(-1)^{l}\bar{p}^{a}\,|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta l}+\frac{(-1)^{k}\,\bar{p}^{a}\,|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta k}}{1+|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta}}

(4.27)

as well as Parts (A) and (B) with
n
as above we prove that
H
is integrable outside some neighbourhood of the origin. This completes the proof of Part (C).

It is enough to prove Part (D) with
m=0
since the generalization to arbitrary
m
is trivial. If
4>\mathsf{d}+2\vartheta
, then
\mathbf{F}H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
and
H
is locally integrable. Otherwise let
k
be the biggest positive integer such that
2k<\mathsf{d}+\vartheta
. Observe that

\mathbf{F}H(\bar{p})=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{l-1}|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta}}{|\bar{p}|^{2l}}+\frac{(-1)^{k}|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta}}{|\bar{p}|^{2k}(1+|\bar{p}|^{2})}.

(4.28)

Using Part (A) and noting that the last term in the above decomposition is in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})
we infer that
H
is locally integrable. Next, we observe

\frac{|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta}}{1+|\bar{p}|^{2}}=|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta}-\frac{|\bar{p}|^{2+\vartheta}}{(1+|\bar{p}|^{2})(1+|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta})}+\frac{|\bar{p}|^{2+2\vartheta}}{(1+|\bar{p}|^{2})(1+|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta})}.

(4.29)

To conclude we apply Part (A) to the first term of the above decomposition and Part (B) to the last two terms with
n
being the smallest integer such that
\mathsf{d}<2n
or
\mathsf{d}+\vartheta<2n
, respectively.

In order to prove Part (E) we first note that
H
is clearly smooth. Next, we observe that

\mathbf{F}H(\bar{p})=\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}~(-1)^{l}p^{a}|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta(l+m)}/l!+p^{a}\,|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta(k+m)}\,f(|\bar{p}|^{\vartheta}),

(4.30)

where
f(t)=t^{-k}(\exp(-t)-\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}~(-t)^{l}/l!)
is a smooth function of fast decay,
n
is the smallest positive integer such that
\vartheta+|a|\leq 2n-\mathsf{d}
and
k
is the smallest positive integer such that
\vartheta(k+m)+|a|>2n-\mathsf{d}
. Part (E) follows now from Part (A) and Part (B) with
n
as above.
∎

5
Function spaces and useful maps

In this section we introduce normed vector spaces for the effective force coefficients and their cumulants. We also define the maps
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
used to write various flow equations that will appear in the paper. We discuss the most important properties of the above-mentioned spaces and maps. We start with the definitions of Besov-type spaces.

Definition 5.1
.

For
\alpha<0
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
we define

\|\phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}:=\sup_{\mu\in(0,1]}[\mu]^{-\alpha}\,\|\bar{K}_{\mu}^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}\ast\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})},

(5.1)

where
{\mathtt{g}}=\lceil-\alpha\rceil
. By definition the space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
is the completion of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
equipped with the norm
\|{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}
.

Remark 5.2
.

The space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
is a separable subspace of the standard Besov space.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\mathtt{w}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R})
. For
v\in C(\mathbb{M})
we set
(\mathtt{w}v)(x):=\mathtt{w}(\mathring{x})\,v(x)
, where
x=(\mathring{x},\bar{x})\in\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}}
. The above definition is extended to
v\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
in a natural way.

Definition 5.4
.

For
\alpha<0
and
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{H})
we define

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H})}:=\sup_{\mu\in(0,1]}[\mu]^{-\alpha}\,\|K_{\mu}^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H})},

(5.2)

where
{\mathtt{g}}=\lceil-\alpha\rceil
. Fix some sequence
\mathtt{w}_{n}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, such that
\mathtt{w}_{n}(\mathring{x})=1
for
\mathring{x}\in[-n,n]
. By definition the space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H})
is a Fréchet space obtained by the completion of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{H})
equipped with the family of seminorms
\|\mathtt{w}_{n}{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H})}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.

Remark 5.5
.

Note that for every
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H})
and every
\mathtt{w}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R})
the distribution
\mathtt{w}f
belongs to the standard parabolic Besov space.

Definition 5.6
.

For
\gamma\in(0,\infty)
and
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
we define

\|\phi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}:=\|\mathbf{R}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})},

(5.3)

where the operator
\mathbf{R}
is given by
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{R}\phi)(\bar{p}):=(1+|\bar{p}|^{\gamma})\mathbf{F}\phi(\bar{p})
. By definition the space
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T})
is the completion of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
equipped with the norm
\|{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}
.

Definition 5.7
.

For
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,
t_{1}<t_{2}
,
\gamma\in(0,\infty)
and
f\in C([t_{1},t_{2}],\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}))
we define

\|\varphi\|_{C([t_{1},t_{2}],\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}))}:=\sup_{\mathring{x}\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\|\varphi(\mathring{x},{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}.

(5.4)

Remark 5.8
.

Recall that
\varepsilon_{\sigma}:=\sigma+1-\lceil\sigma\rceil>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\sigma})
. In what follows, we always assume that
\gamma=\gamma_{\varepsilon}=\sigma-\varepsilon
. Note that the definition of the pseudo-differential operator
\mathbf{R}
given above coincides with the one in Def.
4.8
and by Lemma
4.24
(C) it holds
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T})\subset C^{\lfloor\gamma\rfloor}(\mathbb{T})
, where
\lfloor\gamma\rfloor=\lceil\sigma\rceil-1
.

We now proceed to the definition of the spaces
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}
,
\mathsf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. If
n=1
, we write
\mathsf{m}=m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}=\mathcal{V}^{m}
. Let us mention that we shall use the norm
\|{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}\|_{\mathcal{V}^{m}}
to bound the effective force coefficients
F^{i,m}_{\nu,\mu}
and the norm
\|{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}}
to bound the cumulants of the effective force coefficients. We explain in more detail how these norms will be used in Remark
5.23
below.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
\mathbb{Y}
be a topological space. We define
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{Y})
to be the space of signed Radon measures
V
on
\mathbb{Y}
with finite total variation
|V|
equipped with the following norm

\|V\|_{\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{Y})}:=|V|(\mathbb{Y})=\int_{\mathbb{Y}}|V(\mathrm{d}y)|.

(5.5)

We make the following identification
\mathscr{M}(\{0\})=\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
\mathsf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
,
m=m_{1}+\ldots+m_{n}
and
V\,:\,\mathbb{M}^{n}\to\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{M}^{m})
. The map
\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}}V\,:\,\mathbb{M}^{n}\to\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{M}^{m})
is defined by the equality

\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}}V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{n}):=V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};(A_{1}+x_{1})\times\ldots\times(A_{n}+x_{n}))

(5.6)

for all Borel sets
A_{q}\subset\mathbb{M}^{m_{q}}
,
q\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, where

A+x:=\{(y_{1}+x,\ldots,y_{m}+x)\,|\,(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})\in A\}

(5.7)

for any
A\subset\mathbb{M}^{m}
and
x\in\mathbb{M}
. If
n=1
we write
\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}}=\mathbf{U}^{m}
.

Remark 5.11
.

The equation defining
\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}}V
can be rewritten as

\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}}V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};\mathrm{d}\mathrm{y}_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{y}_{n})=V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{y}_{1}+x_{1})\ldots\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{y}_{n}+x_{n})),

(5.8)

where we use the notation
\mathrm{y}+x:=(y_{1}+x,\ldots,y_{n}+x)\in\mathbb{M}^{m}
for arbitrary
x\in\mathbb{M}
and
\mathrm{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})\in\mathbb{M}^{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Definition 5.12
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
\mathsf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
and
m=m_{1}+\ldots+m_{n}
. The vector space
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}
consists of maps
V\,:\,\mathbb{M}^{n}\to\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{M}^{m})
such that the map
\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}}V
belongs to
C(\mathbb{H}^{n},\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{M}^{m}))
and the norm

\|V\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}}:=\sup_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{M}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{M}^{m}}|V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};\mathrm{d}y_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}y_{m})|\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}\ldots\mathrm{d}x_{n}

(5.9)

is finite. The vector space
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathrm{t}}
consists of
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}
such that
\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}}V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}}V(x_{1}+z,\ldots,x_{n}+z)
for all
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},z\in\mathbb{M}
. If
n=1
we write
\mathsf{m}=m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}=\mathcal{V}^{m}
,
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathrm{t}}=\mathcal{V}^{m}_{\mathrm{t}}
.

Remark 5.13
.

V\in C(\mathbb{H}^{n},\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{M}^{m}))
iff
V\,:\,\mathbb{M}^{n}\to\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{M}^{m})
is continuous and for all
x_{p},z_{p}\in\mathbb{M}
such that
\mathring{x}_{p}=\mathring{z}_{p}
and
\bar{x}_{p}-\bar{z}_{p}\in(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{\mathsf{d}}
,
p\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and all Borel sets
A_{q}\subset\mathbb{M}^{m_{q}}
,
q\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
we have

V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{n})=V(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n};A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{n}).

(5.10)

Remark 5.14
.

Let
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}
. The equality

\langle\tilde{V},\psi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{M}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{M}^{m}}V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};\mathrm{d}y_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}y_{m})\,\psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}x_{n}

(5.11)

for all
\psi\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
defines a distribution
\tilde{V}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
. In what follows, we implicitly identify
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}
with
V\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
and view the space
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}
as a subset of
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
. We use this identification to define
\mathrm{supp}\,V\subset\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m}
. We warn the reader that in what follows
\mathrm{supp}\,V
does not coincide with the support of the map
V\,:\,\mathbb{M}^{n}\to\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{M}^{m})
.

Definition 5.15
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
V\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{k})
. We say that
V
is translationally invariant iff

\langle V,\psi_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\psi_{k}\rangle=\langle V,\psi_{1}({\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}+x)\otimes\ldots\otimes\psi_{k}({\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}+x)\rangle

(5.12)

for any
x\in\mathbb{M}
and
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{k}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
.

Remark 5.16
.

Note that
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}
is an element of
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathrm{t}}
iff
V
is translationally invariant. For
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathrm{t}}
it holds

\|V\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}}=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\sup_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{H}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{M}^{m}}|V(x_{\pi(1)},\ldots,x_{\pi(n)};\mathrm{d}y_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}y_{m})|\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}\ldots\mathrm{d}x_{n},

(5.13)

where
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is the group of permutations of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Remark 5.17
.

Let us note that in our analysis we will only use the norm
\|V\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}}
in a situation when
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathrm{t}}
is translationally invariant, or
n=1
and
\mathsf{m}=m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
V\in\mathcal{V}^{m}
. For this reason the fact that the norm
\|V\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}}
does not treat
x_{1}
and
x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
on equal footing is innocuous.

Definition 5.18
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
{\mathtt{g}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\nu,\mu\in[0,\infty)
the kernel
K^{n,m;{\mathtt{g}}}_{\nu,\mu}\in\mathcal{K}^{n+m}
is defined by

K^{n,m;{\mathtt{g}}}_{\nu,\mu}:=(K^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mu})^{\otimes n}\otimes(K^{\ast{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mu}\ast J_{\nu}^{\phantom{{\mathtt{g}}}})^{\otimes m}.

(5.14)

We also set
K^{m;{\mathtt{g}}}_{\nu,\mu}:=K^{1,m;{\mathtt{g}}}_{\nu,\mu}\in\mathcal{K}^{1+m}
. We omit the indices
\nu,\mu
if
\nu=\mu=1
.

Definition 5.19
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
\mathsf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
,
m=m_{1}+\ldots+m_{n}
. For
\mathtt{w}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
V\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
we define
\mathtt{w}V\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
by the equality
\langle\mathtt{w}V,\psi\rangle:=\langle V,\mathtt{w}\psi\rangle
for all
\psi\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
, where

(\mathtt{w}\psi)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}):=\mathtt{w}(\mathring{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathring{x}_{n})\,\psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}).

(5.15)

For
V\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
we define
\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}}V\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
by the equality
\langle\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}}V,\psi\rangle:=\langle V,\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}\dagger}\psi\rangle
for all
\psi\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
, where

(\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}\dagger}\psi)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};\mathrm{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{y}_{n}):=\psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};\mathrm{y}_{1}-x_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{y}_{n}-x_{n})

(5.16)

and we used the notation introduced in Remark
5.11
.

Definition 5.20
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
\mathsf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
,
m=m_{1}+\ldots+m_{n}
. For
{\mathtt{g}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the space
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{m};{\mathtt{g}}}
consists of distributions
V\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
such that
K^{n,m;{\mathtt{g}}}\ast\mathtt{w}V\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}
for all
\mathtt{w}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathrm{supp}\,\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{m}}V\subset\mathbb{M}^{n}\times([-T,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{d}})^{m}
for some
T\in(0,\infty)
. If
n=1
we set
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{m};{\mathtt{g}}}=\mathcal{D}^{m;{\mathtt{g}}}
. If
n=1
and
m=0
we set
\mathcal{D}^{0;{\mathtt{g}}}=\mathcal{D}^{;{\mathtt{g}}}
. The space
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{m}}\subset\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
is the union of the spaces
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{m};{\mathtt{g}}}
,
{\mathtt{g}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. The spaces
\mathcal{D}^{m}
and
\mathcal{D}
are defined analogously. The spaces
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{m};{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mathrm{t}}
,
\mathcal{D}^{m;{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mathrm{t}}
,
\mathcal{D}^{{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mathrm{t}}
,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathrm{t}}
,
\mathcal{D}^{m}_{\mathrm{t}}
,
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{t}}
consists of translationally invariant elements of the respective spaces defined above.

Remark 5.21
.

For
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}
and
K\in\mathcal{K}^{n+m}
it holds
\|K\ast V\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}}\leq\|K\|_{\mathcal{K}^{n+m}}\|V\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}}
.

Remark 5.22
.

Observe that
\mathcal{D}^{;0}=C(\mathbb{H})
and
\mathcal{V}=C_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{H})
. Moreover, we have
\|v\|_{\mathcal{V}}=\|v\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{M})}
for any
v\in\mathcal{V}
. Using the fact that the kernel
K
is the inverse of a differential operator it is easy to see that
\mathcal{D}=\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H})
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{t}}=\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{t}}\simeq\mathbb{R}
. We also note that for
V\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}^{n}\times\mathbb{M}^{m})
such that
K^{n,m;{\mathtt{g}}}\ast V\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}
we have
K^{n,m;{\mathtt{g}}}\ast\mathtt{w}V\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{m}}
for all
\mathtt{w}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R})
. The last claim is proved by induction on
{\mathtt{g}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
(see related Lemma
9.10
).

Remark 5.23
.

Recall that the kernels
K
